Physical Data

5 Physical Data

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why can a large archive of successful demonstrations leave a robot unable to recover from its first mistake?

A successful demonstration shows how one machine completed a task under one set of conditions. It shows almost nothing about what to do after a slip, a shifted fixture, or a worn gripper, yet a learned policy’s first error carries it into exactly those states, where the archive holds no instruction. Repeating the demonstration enlarges the archive without filling that gap. The recorded motion also carries the operator’s delay, the machine’s limits, and every correction the permission path applied, so a training process that cannot separate these influences learns a safeguard’s intervention or a mechanical limitation as if it were skill. Gathering the missing experience costs operator time and component life, and the collection itself must stay within the machine’s physical limits. Physical data must therefore be engineered, from the choice of experiences to the meaning, timing, and physical origin of every label and the conditions the archive leaves uncovered. Because every collected action crosses the causal boundary, its irreversible costs decide what a machine can learn and where its evidence stops.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain how endogenous covariate shift yields the conditional quadratic imitation-cost bound, and distinguish dataset occupancy from evaluated closed-loop competence
  • Select the action tap a dataset imitates and align multi-rate sensor and action streams to hardware exposure-midpoint timestamps within the task’s spatial tolerance
  • Estimate usable demonstration yield from collection time, operator rest, and physical reset overhead, and budget the mechanical component wear a campaign consumes
  • Identify collection gaps by joining empirical state-action occupancy against a required scenario table
  • Separate failed autonomous actions from admissible human recovery across the divergence, takeover, and stabilization timestamps of an intervention episode
  • Evaluate whether an embodiment’s mechanical, sensing, and timing assumptions transfer to another machine without falling into the normalization fallacy
  • Detect silent calibration and plant drift in a demonstration archive using kinematic reprojection and repeated excitation trajectories
  • Design an immutable provenance record linking each observation to its four-stream action taps (\(\RequestedActionTap, \MappedCommandTap, \EnforcedCommandTap, \MeasuredResponseTap\)) and to the collecting plant
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

5.1 Endogenous Experience

In most machine learning, the dataset exists before the engineering begins. A text corpus or an image benchmark is gathered independently of the model, costs little per additional example, and does not change in response to anything the model predicts. A physical machine has no such corpus. Collecting demonstrations on the robot consumes operator time and component life; robot-free and synthetic sources avoid that robot wear but still require qualification for the target machine. Every telemetry record reflects who commanded the action, what the permission path admitted, and how the physical body moved. Because each executed action changes the physical state of the system, it dictates what subsequent observations can reveal. Physical data is therefore an engineered artifact. The central challenge of physical data engineering is deciding which experiences to collect, and what to record about them, so that a policy learns the task rather than the idiosyncrasies of its collection. Answering this challenge requires four elements: accounting for collection costs in operator time and hardware wear, defining an unambiguous meaning for every recorded action label, establishing a metric for state-space coverage independent of the archive’s internal contents, and explicitly auditing what the archive omits.

Consider a warehouse mobile manipulator tasked with opening a spring-latched cage door. The robot can flawlessly enforce its low-level motion limits and yet fail because it lacks the operational experience to unlatch the door. During a demonstration, a human operator commands motion, the safety permission path checks and filters it, and the robot’s physical body drives against the mechanical latch. The onboard sensor log captures the output of that entire coupled loop. The limits and handoffs recorded earlier for this machine, in the limit record of The six elements of a limit record and the handoff record of The Handoffs Matrix, therefore become preconditions on the training data fed to the learned policy proposer. Every logged command was shaped by them before the body moved. Choosing which physical experiences to collect demands a rigorous accounting of how control inputs shift future sensory states. Choosing what to imitate requires cleanly separating the operator’s raw request from the safety-filtered command and the measured physical response.

A policy fit by supervised imitation to nominal, clean teleoperated demonstrations of door openings (behavioral cloning, defined below) can execute flawlessly in simulation yet jam the latch during its first autonomous deployment. The primary culprit is the distribution shift that the machine’s own closed-loop actions induce. Unlike passive inference on a fixed benchmark, an action executed within an embodied Markov decision process (MDP), in which the next state depends on the current state and action, physically alters the robot’s spatial trajectory, camera viewpoint, and contact states. That displacement changes the probability distribution of every subsequent sensory observation (\(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \sim \EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState \mid \DecisionProcessState_{t}, \CognitivePolicyAction_{t})\)).

↪ The Markov decision process, with its transition model and reward, is developed for the latch task in The latch task as a reward problem.

Definition 5.1: Endogenous covariate shift

Endogenous covariate shift is the closed-loop distribution discrepancy wherein an embodied agent’s executed actions alter its subsequent physical states and sensory observations (\(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \sim \EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState \mid \DecisionProcessState_{t}, \CognitivePolicyAction_{t})\)), driving the system into state-space regimes unsupported by its training demonstrations.

  1. Significance: Explains how offline behavioral cloning can fail during closed-loop physical execution: a perturbation may drive the system into states where the policy lacks training support.
  2. Distinction: Unlike exogenous dataset shift caused by changing ambient lighting or seasonal weather, endogenous shift is actively generated by the agent’s own closed-loop actions altering its subsequent observation distribution.
  3. Common pitfall: Attempting to fix closed-loop policy failure by collecting more nominal expert demonstrations, which only increases data density along the success manifold without providing the corrective recovery data needed to escape boundary excursions.

This distribution shift is how the second law, endogenous data (principle 2), shows up in data collection, where the machine’s own actions choose the states its archive must later answer for. An archive composed exclusively of flawless executions without recovery trajectories becomes an operational liability. The moment the autonomous policy commits its first sub-millimeter tracking error, the robot enters states never encountered during training. Two distinct physical mechanisms govern how fast that departure grows (section 5.2).

5.2 Mechanics of Endogenous Covariate Shift

When an image classifier misidentifies a dog as a cat, the mistake does not alter the pixels of the next photograph in the test set. When a door-opening policy misses the latch centerline by a millimeter, that motion shifts the camera viewpoint, changes the contact geometry, and alters every observation that follows. Classical empirical risk minimization assumes the first setting, drawing independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) samples from an exogenous, stationary distribution \(\ExogenousDataDistribution\). Even when passive machine learning studies covariate shift, it treats the shift as an open-loop observational change: the marginal input distribution \(\InputDistribution\) shifts (such as evaluating a daytime-trained vision model at night) while the conditional data-generating function \(\ConditionalDistribution\) stays fixed. Because a passive model’s predictions \(\PassiveModelPrediction\) exert no physical back-action on the world, an inference error never mutates the next input. Imitation learning on a machine operates inside the closed-loop counterpart to this setting.

↩︎ The closed loop through which a flawed proposal carries the machine into states missing from its training distribution is established in From a proposed action to motion.

In an embodied physical system, two distinct mechanisms distort what the learner encounters. The first shifts the states that the policy visits even when the hardware stays unchanged; the second alters how the physical plant responds to any given control input:

  1. Endogenous state distribution shift: The policy’s executed actions \(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} = \Policy(\MultimodalObservation_{t})\) directly drive the physical state transition \(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \sim \EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState \mid \DecisionProcessState_{t}, \CognitivePolicyAction_{t})\), choosing the sensory observations \(\MultimodalObservation_{t+1}\) the robot sees next. A single control error moves the body into an unvisited region of the state space and changes the incoming observation distribution on the next tick.
  2. Active physical plant drift: The physical plant is a continuous dynamical system governed by differential equations of motion \(\PlantStateRate = \PlantDynamicsFunction(\MechanicalPlantState, \ControlInput; \PlantDynamicsParameters(\TimeInstant))\) whose parameters drift over operational time. Actuator Joule heating (\(\TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature)\)) raises winding temperature and copper resistance via \(\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature) = \ElectricalResistance_0(1 + \CopperTemperatureCoefficient \JouleanTemperatureRise)\), compressing the motor drive’s voltage headroom and capping peak torque at high joint speeds. Over extended operating cycles, continuous loading degrades strain-wave gearbox lubrication, lowering torsional stiffness and expanding mechanical lost motion. Compliant elastomer gripper pads wear down and deform, shrinking the Coulomb friction cone (\(\lVert \TangentialContactShear \rVert \le \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \NormalContactForce\)). Finally, physical workpieces vary in mass, center of gravity, and surface texture across trials, perturbing both the inertial loads the arm accelerates and the gravitational torques the joints sustain.

Together, the policy’s closed-loop actions and the plant’s response induce an endogenous state visitation distribution \(\LearnerStateVisitationDistribution(\DecisionProcessState)\), the expected fraction of time steps that a rollout of policy \(\pi\) spends in state \(\DecisionProcessState\). When an agent is trained by offline behavioral cloning on demonstrations from an expert policy \(\pi^*\), empirical risk minimization fits the imitation objective only over the expert’s occupancy distribution \(\ExpertStateVisitationDistribution(\DecisionProcessState)\). During autonomous execution, the learned policy instead acts under its own induced distribution \(\LearnerStateVisitationDistribution(\DecisionProcessState)\). Wherever \(\LearnerStateVisitationDistribution\) departs from \(\ExpertStateVisitationDistribution\), the policy encounters states that hold no demonstrated corrective action.

Definition 5.2: Behavioral cloning

Behavioral cloning is imitation learning posed as supervised learning: a policy is fit to map each recorded observation to the action the demonstrator took there, with the loss weighted by the demonstrator’s own state occupancy \(\ExpertStateVisitationDistribution\).

  1. Significance: Training error is measured only on states the demonstrator visited, while deployment cost accrues on the states the learned policy visits. Under a 0–1 action loss (one whenever the policy’s action differs from the expert’s, zero otherwise) the worst-case excess cost can therefore grow with the square of the horizon \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\), the episode length in control steps, rather than linearly, as the next section derives.
  2. Distinction: Unlike corrective aggregation, which obtains expert labels at states the learner itself reaches (section 5.8 develops it), behavioral cloning never observes the consequences of its own mistakes; unlike reinforcement learning, it needs demonstrations but no reward.
  3. Common pitfall: Reading a low held-out loss as evidence of closed-loop competence. A held-out split drawn from the same demonstrations shares their occupancy, so it cannot contain the states the policy’s first error reaches.

Because behavioral cloning weights its loss solely by the demonstrator’s occupancy \(\ExpertStateVisitationDistribution\), a single step off the demonstrated path places the policy in states where training never penalized an error. How fast that first departure compounds over an episode depends on whether the archive holds any actions that steer the machine back (section 5.3).

5.3 Recovery and Compounding Error

Opening a latch from a nominal centered approach and recovering from an off-axis sideways slip require different physical actions (figure 1). Every retained demonstration guides the gripper forward along the narrow corridor centered on the strike plate, which populates the archive with thousands of forward-progress actions. The archive contains zero demonstrations that start from a gripper displaced a few millimeters toward the fixture and return it to the centerline. Because a skilled human operator rarely lets the gripper drift into those boundary regions, and because teleoperation runs that drifted noticeably were aborted or discarded during curation, nothing in the training data constrains the policy’s output at such a displaced state.1 The recoverability problem is this absence of demonstrated corrective actions that steer the system back into the nominal corridor.

1 ALVINN (Autonomous Land Vehicle in a Neural Network): Pomerleau’s road-following network learned to steer from a human driver who kept the van centered, so its training images held almost no off-center views (Pomerleau 1989). His remedy shifted each camera image sideways in software and corrected the steering label to match, synthesizing recovery examples about 20 years before Dataset Aggregation (DAgger, section 5.8) (Pomerleau 1991). A warp could fill that gap because the missing state was only a viewpoint of a flat road, whereas a gripper displaced toward the fixture also lacks contact forces that no image transform can manufacture, so its recoveries must be demonstrated or collected.

Pomerleau, Dean A. 1989. “ALVINN: An Autonomous Land Vehicle in a Neural Network.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 1: 305–13.
Pomerleau, Dean A. 1991. “Efficient Training of Artificial Neural Networks for Autonomous Navigation.” Neural Computation 3 (1): 88–97. https://doi.org/10.1162/neco.1991.3.1.88.

Whether this missing return action leads to task failure depends on the closed-loop dynamics of the complete physical plant. Because the robot’s physical state encompasses joint coordinates, joint velocities, motor drive currents, contact wrenches (combined three-axis contact forces and torques), and raw camera streams, a nominal demonstration traces an infinitesimally thin manifold through a high-dimensional state space. Almost any unmodeled disturbance displaces the system off this path. If the physical plant and its low-level joint controllers inherently restore small lateral displacements toward the centerline through compliant passive dynamics, an imperfect policy output near the corridor boundary will naturally attenuate. If the system dynamics lack this self-centering stability, the tracking error will persist or compound until a learned recovery policy, the safety permission path, or a human supervisor intervenes. While production demonstration archives often contain occasional human corrections, their coverage cannot be assumed; it must be audited against an explicit scenario table (section 5.7).

The quadratic worst-case scaling of behavioral cloning can be derived through a discrete summation over horizon steps. Suppose an autonomous agent follows the expert trajectory until encountering its first execution error at step \(\FirstErrorStepIndex \in \{1, \dots, \BehaviorCloningHorizon\}\). If this error propels the system into an unsupported state from which it never recovers, the agent incurs an adverse task cost at every remaining step (\(\BehaviorCloningHorizon - \FirstErrorStepIndex\)). Assigning each time step an upper bound \(\SingleStepImitationError\) on the probability of committing a first error and normalizing the per-step cost to unity (\(\ImmediateTaskCostScore_t = 1\)) yields the following worst-case cost accumulation. This discrete summation serves as an upper-bound derivation rather than an exact expectation over independent failure events: \[\sum_{\FirstErrorStepIndex=1}^\BehaviorCloningHorizon \SingleStepImitationError (\BehaviorCloningHorizon - \FirstErrorStepIndex) = \SingleStepImitationError \left( \BehaviorCloningHorizon^2 - \sum_{\FirstErrorStepIndex=1}^\BehaviorCloningHorizon \FirstErrorStepIndex \right) = \SingleStepImitationError \left( \BehaviorCloningHorizon^2 - \frac{\BehaviorCloningHorizon(\BehaviorCloningHorizon+1)}{2} \right) = \frac{\BehaviorCloningHorizon(\BehaviorCloningHorizon-1)}{2} \SingleStepImitationError \approx \frac{1}{2} \BehaviorCloningHorizon^2 \SingleStepImitationError.\] In open-loop supervised learning, by contrast, if per-step inference errors were independent and exerted zero feedback on subsequent model inputs, an error rate of \(\SingleStepImitationError\) per step would scale linearly over \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\) steps as \(\sum_{t=1}^\BehaviorCloningHorizon \SingleStepImitationError = \BehaviorCloningHorizon\SingleStepImitationError\). The extra factor of \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\) appearing in \(\CovariateDriftBound\) is the mathematical signature of closed-loop endogenous feedback.

This quadratic bound characterizes the accumulation of task cost over time, not the physical trajectory of the machine. The result does not imply that spatial tracking errors grow quadratically as \(\TimeInstant^2\). Closed-loop contact forces, mechanical compliance, and actuator saturation govern how physical trajectories evolve; the derivation formalizes only the cost of operating in states where the policy lacks training support.

Writing \(\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi)\) and \(\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*)\) for the expected cumulative task costs of the learned policy \(\pi\) and expert policy \(\pi^*\) over those \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\) steps, let \(\SingleStepImitationError\) bound the single-step action disagreement across expert-visited states under a 0–1 action loss: \(\mathbb{E}_{\DecisionProcessState \sim \ExpertStateVisitationDistribution} [\IndicatorFunction(\pi(\DecisionProcessState) \ne \pi^*(\DecisionProcessState))] \le \SingleStepImitationError\). Assuming per-step task costs lie in \([0,1]\), behavioral cloning admits the worst-case bound (equation 1) derived by Ross and Bagnell (2010) and restated by Ross et al. (2011): \[\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi)-\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*) \le \BehaviorCloningHorizon^2\SingleStepImitationError. \tag{1}\] The published theorem carries a constant of one rather than the sketch’s one half; both are worst-case bounds, and their quadratic dependence on the horizon is the same.

In practice, this theoretical bound can exceed the maximum possible cost achievable in a finite episode. Because per-step costs are bounded in \([0,1]\), the excess cost is trivially bounded by \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\); hence, \(\min(\BehaviorCloningHorizon, \BehaviorCloningHorizon^2\SingleStepImitationError)\) represents the operational upper bound. Once the quadratic expression surpasses \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\), it provides no meaningful prediction regarding physical failure rates or contact severity. The practical engineering value of this bound lies in exposing why behavioral cloning has no defense in states its demonstrations never reached, rather than in predicting exact physical collision rates.

↪ The full derivation of the quadratic bound, with its loss and cost assumptions, is developed in Imitation Learning and Compounding Covariate Shift.

Foster, Dylan J., Adam Block, and Dipendra Misra. 2024. “Is Behavior Cloning All You Need? Understanding Horizon in Imitation Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 37: 120602–66. https://doi.org/10.52202/079017-3834.
Simchowitz, Max, Daniel Pfrommer, and Ali Jadbabaie. 2025. “The Pitfalls of Imitation Learning When Actions Are Continuous.” Proceedings of the Thirty Eighth Conference on Learning Theory (COLT), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 291: 5248–351.

The actual scaling with horizon length depends heavily on the chosen loss function, the action space, and the closed-loop plant stability. Consequently, equation 1 reflects a classical result for discrete 0–1 loss rather than an absolute law for all imitation architectures. When trained under logarithmic loss (minimizing the negative log-probability assigned to expert actions) over a well-specified policy class containing the expert, behavioral cloning can achieve a sample complexity that does not grow with the horizon length. This result holds provided total reward is bounded and the policy class capacity is controlled (Foster et al. 2024). Continuous action spaces, however, introduce the opposite challenge. Even when the physical closed loop is exponentially stable (small mechanical perturbations decay at a fixed rate) and the expert policy is smooth and deterministic, any smooth, deterministic imitator can accumulate closed-loop execution errors that scale exponentially with horizon length relative to its training loss (Simchowitz et al. 2025). The same analysis identifies two ways around this compounding (Simchowitz et al. 2025). The first changes the policy class. A policy that is non-smooth, that is non-Markovian (its action depends on recent history rather than solely the current observation), or whose action distribution is multimodal (it can place probability on several distinct actions at once, rather than averaging them) escapes the result. Action chunking and diffusion decoders have these properties. The second changes the data, using demonstrations deliberately spread across a wide tube surrounding the nominal trajectory. That second remedy is a data-collection decision, and section 5.8 returns to it as noise injection during teleoperation. Because the mobile manipulator operates in a continuous control space, closed-loop success ultimately hinges on whether the combined policy and low-level controllers actively restore a displaced gripper to the nominal path.

↩︎ Action chunking, in which the policy emits a short sequence of setpoints per inference, is established in Action chunking.

Collecting more nominal expert demonstrations does not resolve endogenous covariate shift. Adding another thousand hours of clean, centered door openings merely increases sample density along the nominal trajectory; the empirical support outside this narrow tube remains strictly zero (\(\ExpertStateVisitationDistribution(\DecisionProcessState) = 0\)). This pitfall is the nominal demonstration trap. Beyond this coverage limitation, logging physical robot experience carries direct hardware costs. While a web crawler scrapes static text with zero marginal mechanical wear, every recorded second of robot telemetry consumes actuator fatigue life, heats motor windings (\(d\WindingTemperature/d\TimeInstant\)), wears down gearbox teeth, and depletes battery reserves. In practical data-collection campaigns, required pauses for operator fatigue, sensor recalibration, scene resets, and discarded trials often leave less than one quarter of scheduled workcell time as usable demonstration data (section 5.6). Physical telemetry is scarce, wear-intensive, and costly; collecting it without modeling closed-loop recovery dynamics compromises policy training.

↩︎ Actuator thermal dissipation (\(d\WindingTemperature/d\TimeInstant\)) and winding damage limits are established in Thermal Duty Cycles.

A physical command also changes meaning as it crosses the control stack toward the actuators. In a system partitioned by the proposal boundary, an operator’s or policy’s requested action is mapped into a trajectory setpoint, checked and potentially clamped by the safety permission path, and executed by the physical plant to produce a measured response. Logging only the raw requested action trains the policy on commands the plant may be physically incapable of executing; logging only the measured plant response teaches the policy to copy link flexure, gear backlash, and contact stalls as if they were intentional human skill. A physical data-collection campaign must therefore settle two decisions before recording its first trajectory. The first is which collection path generates the actions (section 5.4). The second is which of the four representations of one command (the request, the mapped setpoint, the admitted command, or the measured response) the dataset preserves as its training label (section 5.5).

↩︎ The proposal boundary, which separates a learned proposer from the permission path that alone may admit an action, is established in The Machine in Five Levels.

5.4 Physical Telemetry Sources and Collection Paths

When an engineer records demonstrations of the warehouse cage-door latch, the choice of collection rig decides which contact forces, hesitations, and recovery maneuvers ever reach the archive. Guiding the robot arm by hand gives the demonstrator direct tactile feel of the binding latch, but the demonstrator’s torso blocks the onboard cameras. Puppeteering the arm over a network clears the camera view, but display delay makes the operator inch forward in a stop-and-go hunt. Running a scripted spline produces clean, repeatable approaches that jam the moment the strike plate shifts by a millimeter. Because every attempt on the physical robot spends facility hours, joint life, and human effort, choosing a collection path trades collection cost against the behaviors the resulting archive can support. Seven collection paths appear in practice, six on physical hardware and a seventh in simulation:

  1. In kinesthetic teaching, a colocated human demonstrator physically grasps and guides the unpowered or gravity-compensated links of the mechanism. The logging pipeline records joint encoder positions at the native controller rate without network transport delays. However, human biomechanics govern the motion’s bandwidth, moving far more slowly than the controller’s sampling loop, while labor costs scale \(1:1\) with demonstrator time. The demonstrator’s body also frequently occludes onboard camera views, introducing systematic visual distribution shifts relative to autonomous operation.
  2. In teleoperation, a remote operator manipulates an input device to puppet the follower robot across a network fabric.2 Teleoperation interfaces differ in how operator inputs map to robot actuators and how much physical contact information reflects back to the human. Joint-space leader-follower arms such as ALOHA (Zhao et al. 2023) and GELLO (Wu et al. 2024) mirror the follower’s joint topology, often at reduced scale, streaming joint angles directly to the follower without inverse kinematics (computing the joint angles that place the tool at a commanded pose). In contrast, tracked spatial controllers and rate-controlled space mice command Cartesian tool poses or velocities that require inverse kinematics solvers at runtime, while force-reflecting bilateral arms actively transmit measured contact forces back to the operator’s hands. Table 2 compares these interfaces and names the artifact each leaves in the recording.
  3. In scripted collection, a deterministic trajectory generator executes automated motion primitives, such as cubic splines or impedance-governed state machines. Ingestion rates are high and predictable (\(50\text{--}500\text{ Hz}\)), but trajectories traverse rigid, repetitive manifolds. Because these paths fail to generate corrective recovery data during off-nominal contact, the manipulator risks jamming whenever workpiece dimensions deviate by even a millimeter.
  4. In exploratory collection, an active neural policy or reinforcement learning agent selects actions to maximize an information-theoretic objective, such as epistemic entropy or intrinsic curiosity. Although data throughput matches the robot’s peak policy cadence (\(50\text{--}100\text{ Hz}\)), unconstrained exploratory actions risk high-energy collisions, tripping hardware safety tripwires or degrading mechanical gearboxes through repeated high-torque shock loads.
  5. In autonomous deployment logging, production policies control actuators during routine factory or warehouse operation, streaming multimodal telemetry into local circular buffers in situ. This channel yields zero-marginal-labor data at the system’s full execution rate (\(10\text{--}50\text{ Hz}\) for high-level policies, \(1\text{ kHz}\) for joint servos). However, runtime safety barriers heavily censor the recorded state distribution, capturing almost exclusively nominal operating regimes while excluding boundary failures.
  6. In robot-free demonstration, the operator carries a hand-held gripper fitted with a wrist camera and performs tasks directly in the physical environment, as in the Universal Manipulation Interface (UMI) (Chi et al. 2024). Collection incurs zero robot operating hours or actuator wear, allowing a single demonstrator to gather data across locations where no robot is deployed. The engineering cost shifts to label synthesis: because the hand-held rig records its own Cartesian trajectory and visual feed without robot joint states, actions must be expressed relative to the gripper’s pose frame and executed downstream through the target robot’s controller. The deployed robot’s observation and actuation latencies must also be calibrated to match the recording timing (section 5.5.2).

2 Bilateral Telemanipulation: Raymond Goertz’s 1949 master-slave manipulator at Argonne National Laboratory moved tongs behind radiation shielding in exact correspondence with a master handle, and its mechanical linkage returned the forces at the tongs to the operator’s hand (Goertz 1949; Hokayem and Spong 2006). Remote manipulation therefore began with contact force inside the loop. A demonstration recorded through an interface that drops that force channel cannot contain the contact corrections the operator would have made by feel.

Goertz, Raymond C. 1949. Master-Slave Manipulator. ANL-4311. Argonne National Laboratory. https://doi.org/10.2172/1054625.
Hokayem, Peter F., and Mark W. Spong. 2006. “Bilateral Teleoperation: An Historical Survey.” Automatica 42 (12): 2035–57. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.automatica.2006.06.027.

A seventh source removes physical hardware entirely. Simulation pipelines eliminate mechanical wear by substituting a numerical contact model for the physical plant, generating synthetic demonstrations at scale. Trajectory generators can expand a few hundred human seed demonstrations across novel object poses and scene layouts, replaying them in physics engines to synthesize tens of thousands of varied trajectories (Mandlekar et al. 2023). Downstream policies can then co-train on simulated and real telemetry at a specified mixing ratio. However, these synthetic streams require rigorous metadata tagging: records must log their source generator, physics engine build, integrator step size, and contact solver parameters. The dataset’s scenario table, the list of required operating conditions that section 5.7.1 builds, must also flag every condition covered solely by synthetic rollouts, ensuring that training diagnostics can isolate simulation artifacts from measured physical plant behavior. The provenance requirements for synthetic telemetry appear under dataset degradation (section 5.10.2).

Because the collection source determines how the machine interacts with its physical environment, these data streams occupy disjoint state-action manifolds even when executing identical manipulation tasks. Consider releasing the warehouse mobile manipulator’s cage-door latch. A colocated demonstrator guiding the arm feels the latch bind through direct tactile resistance and immediately adjusts wrist compliance, whereas a remote teleoperator watching a compressed video stream lacks haptic feedback and falls into step-and-wait hunting, pausing repeatedly to check strike-plate contact. By contrast, a scripted controller advances along a fixed geometric path at constant velocity and produces the same rigid contact-force spike on every repetition, while an exploratory policy approaches at oblique angles that bind the latch until safety tripwires abort the trial. Far from representing noisy variations of a single ground-truth trajectory, these recorded streams reflect distinct dynamical systems with different bandwidths, transport latencies, and mechanical compliance profiles, as summarized in table 1.

The cheapest sources in table 1 also produce the highest volume of records. This abundance frequently invites a misconception borrowed from disembodied machine learning, where empirical scaling laws correlate model capability directly with raw token count or pretraining dataset volume (\(\RawRecordCount\)). In physical telemetry, treating every logged control step as an independent observation is a category error. An edge logger sampling at 1 kHz records 3.6 × 10⁶ state-action pairs every hour, yet adjacent timesteps carry near-total temporal autocorrelation (\(\SerialAutocorrelation \to 1.0\)) because successive kinematic readings sampled 1 ms apart differ only by sub-millimeter structural displacements and sensor quantization noise. For a stationary scalar telemetry statistic with geometrically decaying autocorrelation \(\SerialAutocorrelation_k = \SerialAutocorrelation^\AutocorrelationLagIndex\), as characterized by a first-order autoregressive AR(1) process, the large-sample effective sample size (ESS)3 for estimating its mean is: \[\EffectiveSampleSize = \RawRecordCount \frac{1 - \SerialAutocorrelation}{1 + \SerialAutocorrelation}. \tag{2}\] Under this autoregressive model, an autocorrelation of \(\SerialAutocorrelation =\) 0.999 across 3.6 × 10⁶ raw records yields only 1,800 statistically independent observations. While lag-1 correlation alone does not govern ESS across every non-stationary telemetry stream, high-frequency sampling still cannot substitute for operational diversity. Whereas consecutive tokens in language modeling represent high-entropy transitions across a large vocabulary, high-rate robot telemetry mostly oversamples the same physical trajectory. It can resolve fast contact transients when they occur, but raising the logger’s clock frequency never populates an unvisited scenario cell. Physical dataset coverage expands instead through independent physical episodes, diverse workpiece placements, varying illumination, and shifting friction coefficients. A curated archive containing several hundred episodes that systematically sweep initial geometric and mechanical conditions provides far richer empirical support than millions of repetitive timesteps collected across a static test bench.

3 Effective Sample Size: The factor \((1-\SerialAutocorrelation)/(1+\SerialAutocorrelation)\) is the effective-sample-size correction for the mean of a first-order autoregressive series, the correction climatologists apply to serially correlated weather records before testing a trend (Thiébaux and Zwiers 1984). At the correlation used here it turns an hour of permission-tick records into about 1,800 independent ones, so a step count overstates the independent information available for estimating this statistic’s mean by three orders of magnitude before coverage is even considered.

Thiébaux, H. J., and Francis W. Zwiers. 1984. “The Interpretation and Estimation of Effective Sample Size.” Journal of Climate and Applied Meteorology 23 (5): 800–811. https://doi.org/10.1175/1520-0450(1984)023<0800:TIAEOE>2.0.CO;2.

5.4.1 Human demonstration idiosyncrasies and mechanical artifacts

When a behavioral cloning policy is trained directly on raw teleoperated joint velocities, the deployed robot arm often chatters its harmonic drives at \(8\text{--}12\text{ Hz}\) or stalls for \(300\text{ ms}\) a few millimeters in front of the latch strike plate. Neither defect comes from the network architecture. Because a human operator executed the training motions, the recorded telemetry embeds the demonstrator’s neuromuscular bursts, perceptual delays, and visual blind spots alongside the intended trajectory:

  • Neuromuscular sub-movements and physiological tremor: Human motor control operates through discrete motor unit recruitment bursts rather than continuous, smooth trajectories. These neuromuscular dynamics introduce corrective sub-movements at \(2\text{--}3\text{ Hz}\) overlaid with involuntary physiological tremor at \(8\text{--}12\text{ Hz}\). If a high-frequency behavioral cloning policy trains on raw joint velocities without low-pass filtering, the neural network imitates this biological tremor as high-frequency torque chatter, accelerating mechanical fatigue in the robot’s harmonic drives and gearboxes.
  • Visual latency and step-and-wait hunting: When an operator guides a manipulator toward a contact fixture using 2D camera monitors, monocular depth ambiguity and video compression latency force an unnatural step-and-wait hunting cycle. The operator inches the gripper forward by a few millimeters, pauses for \(200\text{--}400\text{ ms}\) to visually confirm clearance or contact across the video stream, and only then resumes motion. An imitation policy trained on uncurated velocity streams treats these latency-induced pauses as deliberate actions, yielding deployed behaviors that stutter, oscillate, or stall mid-trajectory.
  • Attentional narrowing and gaze-conditioned bias: Human demonstrators naturally focus visual attention on the active tool center point (TCP). Because the operator rarely tracks the peripheral links of the arm during fine manipulation, the robot’s elbow or shoulder links frequently sweep perilously close to structural supports or table edges. An imitation policy trained on these demonstrations inherits trajectories that graze workspace obstacles, yet lacks the human’s peripheral situational awareness or rapid collision avoidance reflexes.

Because an ingestion logger records every physical tremor, visual hesitation, and marginal joint clearance as part of the demonstration, imitation policies reproduce these flaws unless the training pipeline filters or abstracts them. Choosing a telemetry source decides what raw experience reaches the archive; choosing which control signal serves as the action label decides whether the policy learns the operator’s intent, the safety filter’s clamp, or the plant’s compliance (section 5.5).

5.5 Action Label Selection across the Control Boundary

Suppose a teleoperator pushes the gripper into the cage-door strike plate aggressively enough to trip a velocity clamp, or stalls against the latch while the wrist motor holds torque. A logger that records only the operator’s raw command teaches the policy to repeat a move the machine refused to execute, whereas a logger that records only the measured encoder velocity teaches it that stalling against a latch is a deliberate command to stop. By the time the gripper contacts the strike plate, a single operator motion has become four different numerical signals across two decoupled processing domains:

  • The unprivileged Linux application processor executes perception pipelines, handles video frame ingestion, runs teleoperation input mapping, and computes the neural policy forward pass (\(\Policy(\MultimodalObservation_{t})\)).
  • On the permission side of the proposal boundary, a hard real-time safety microcontroller (MCU) runs joint and permission checks at the chosen 1 kHz cadence. Dedicated motor-drive logic closes the faster field-oriented current loop; its current samples remain distinct from the telemetry retained at each permission tick.4

4 Hardware Input Capture: On real-time MCUs, a timer’s input-capture unit latches encoder counts and current-shunt conversions at a hardware edge, and Direct Memory Access (DMA) moves them to memory without waiting on software. Sampling jitter stays within microseconds, so the denominator of a numerically differentiated velocity or acceleration carries no scheduling noise.

5.5.1 The four-stream action tap tuple

If a dataset keeps only one signal from those two processing domains, any discrepancy between what the operator asked for and how the arm actually moved becomes impossible to diagnose. A command must therefore be logged at each of the four causal stages it passes through. The four-stream action tap \((\RequestedActionTap, \MappedCommandTap, \EnforcedCommandTap, \MeasuredResponseTap)\) records all four in one hardware-synchronized telemetry log (figure 2). Two are logged on the unprivileged Linux host before the black-channel link, and two on the discrete safety MCU after the 1 kHz permission check. Logged together, the taps keep interventions and tracking errors auditable.

Dividing telemetry logging across the unprivileged Linux host and the hard real-time MCU captures four distinct physical signals:

  • Tap 1: The requested action (\(\RequestedActionTap\), “Asked”): Logged in user space on the application processor, this represents unconstrained human operator input (from a leader arm or joystick) or the raw forward-pass output from an unverified policy, tagged with its native coordinate frame and issue timestamp prior to software validation. This proposal reflects unadulterated human or policy intent, but it frequently demands infeasible joint accelerations or violates spatial bounding envelopes.
  • Tap 2: The mapped command (\(\MappedCommandTap\), “Mapped”): The kinematically retargeted setpoint (such as Cartesian end-effector velocities converted to joint space setpoints via inverse kinematics), incorporating coordinate frame transformations, trajectory interpolation, and workspace limits before transmission across the black-channel board link to the safety MCU.
  • Tap 3: The enforced command (\(\EnforcedCommandTap\), “Allowed”): Logged on the bare-metal MCU on the permission side of the boundary, this represents the command after the permission path has checked or modified the proposal. The checks include deterministic Control Barrier Functions (CBFs, safety filters that keep states within a forward-invariant safe set), velocity and acceleration clamps, torque saturation envelopes, and collision guards. Whichever tap a dataset chooses as its label, the imitation loss has the same form; written for the admitted command, it is: \[\PolicyImitationLoss(\ModelParameters) = \mathbb{E}_{(\MultimodalObservation_{t}, \EnforcedCommandLabel) \sim \DemonstrationDataset} \left[ \LossFunction(\Policy(\MultimodalObservation_{t}), \EnforcedCommandLabel) \right],\] Here the training dataset must contain curated, correctly attributed targets. Passing the permission check does not make a command an expert demonstration, because a clamp or emergency stop may be a safeguard’s response to a poor request. Override flags and companion taps determine which records can serve as imitation targets and which remain evidence of intervention.
  • Tap 4: The measured response (\(\MeasuredResponseTap\), “Done”): The physical plant reaction measured by optical joint encoders and motor current shunt resistors over the executed control tick, recording actual mechanical displacement and electrical effort. This measured response serves as proprioceptive state input (\(\NextProprioceptiveObservation\)) for subsequent policy steps rather than an action label.

↪ Control barrier functions and the forward-invariant safe sets they enforce are developed in Safe Sets as Conditional Permission.

These distinctions become critical when commanded intent and physical motion diverge. If a teleoperator requests an aggressive movement that the safety permission path clamps, the resulting state transition follows the enforced command (\(\EnforcedCommandTap\)), not the unconstrained request (\(\RequestedActionTap\)). Pairing that transition only with the request conceals the safety intervention and teaches a policy to repeat demands the physical machine cannot execute.

At the latch, the opposite ambiguity arises. Contact resistance can bring measured joint velocities to zero (\(\MeasuredJointVelocities \approx \ZeroVector\)) while the motors continue exerting holding torque against the strike plate. Treating that response as a deliberate zero-velocity target loses the distinction between maintaining effort against the latch and choosing to stop. The appropriate imitation target depends on the policy’s action interface; the other taps explain how that target became physical motion.

Retaining all four streams is what makes any single choice of label interpretable. A policy trained on the request alone learns commands the permission path would have clamped, and one trained on the measured response alone learns the arm’s compliance, backlash, and external disturbances as if they were intended control. A log that keeps only the final encoder state or only the operator’s input discards exactly the stages at which software and safety hardware changed the command. The tuple is also only as reliable as its shared clock. The application processor and the MCU must stamp their taps in one hardware-synchronized timebase, such as the IEEE 1588 Precision Time Protocol (PTP), written \(\PtpHardwareTimestamp\). A few milliseconds of skew between them misaligns each visual observation from the torque it prompted (section 5.5.2).

For the latch campaign, the imitation target is the mapped command whenever it represents an expert request that the permission path accepted unchanged. In that case \(\MappedCommandTap\) and \(\EnforcedCommandTap\) carry the same value, so the choice between the two taps matters only for commands the permission path modified. A modified command remains a separate, attributed intervention; a curated recovery can later supply an expert target at that state. Each training pair therefore carries two times. The observation is dated when its physics was true, at the exposure midpoint (the instant halfway through the interval over which the camera integrates light), and the command is dated when the operator issued it. The gap between them is part of the demonstration and is recorded rather than erased, because it contains the operator’s own reaction time (section 5.6.1). The alignment developed below supplies the first of these times.

↪ The capture timestamp, taken in hardware at the exposure midpoint, is enforced for every sensor in Dating and Placing the Measurement.

5.5.2 Multi-rate time synchronization and exposure-midpoint alignment

Recording the right action tap still leaves open when each recorded observation was physically true, because a robot’s sensors run at different rates and transport delays. A naive data loader that latches the latest available sensor sample pairs each action with a camera frame whose optical exposure was centered \(25\text{--}40\text{ ms}\) before the frame arrived in host memory, recording that delayed image as the present state. Trained on those pairs, the policy learns to map historical visual states to present actions, baking an artificial phase lag into its weights that shows up at deployment as overshoot and chattering against the latch. Across the machine, cameras integrate photons over exposure windows of \(10\text{--}35\text{ ms}\) at \(30\text{--}60\text{ Hz}\), 6-axis force-torque sensors stream over a Serial Peripheral Interface (SPI) or Controller Area Network (CAN) bus at \(100\text{--}500\text{ Hz}\), joint and permission telemetry is retained at the MCU’s chosen \(1\text{ kHz}\) tick while motor-drive current sampling serves the faster local current loop, and neural policy models dispatch setpoints at \(10\text{--}50\text{ Hz}\). Unlike the operator’s reaction time, this pairing error belongs to the logging pipeline rather than the demonstration, so stamping each sample at its physical capture instant lets the alignment pipeline remove it.

To quantify the physical hazard of temporal misalignment, consider the spatial tracking error \(\SyncPairingDisplacement\) injected into the dataset when an observation and an action are paired across an uncompensated phase lag \(\ObservationPairingLag\): \[\SyncPairingDisplacement(\TimeInstant) = \int_{t - \ObservationPairingLag}^{\TimeInstant} \Velocity(\IntegrationDummyTime)\,d\IntegrationDummyTime \approx \Velocity(\TimeInstant) \cdot \ObservationPairingLag. \tag{3}\] The latch campaign moves far more slowly than an arm in transit, and notebook 5.1 applies equation 3 at both speeds.

Napkin Math 5.1: The pairing lag
Problem: A logger pairs each action with a camera frame whose exposure midpoint lies an uncompensated 35 ms earlier. Does the resulting pairing error fit the 2 mm latch clearance at transit speed and at the latch campaign’s two approach speeds, and how much lag can each approach tolerate?

Variables:

  • Uncompensated lag \(\ObservationPairingLag =\) 35 ms (illustrative; exposure-to-arrival delay of an untimed logger)
  • Latch clearance \(\LatchClearance =\) 2 mm (illustrative)
  • Transit speed 0.50 m/s (illustrative); guarded approach 0.03 m/s and faster approach 0.10 m/s (illustrative scenario speeds of the running machine)

Math:

  1. Transit: 0.50 m/s \(\times\) 35 ms \(=\) 17.5 mm, 8.8× the clearance.
  2. Guarded approach: 0.03 m/s \(\times\) 35 ms \(=\) 1.05 mm, inside the clearance.
  3. Faster approach: 0.10 m/s \(\times\) 35 ms \(=\) 3.5 mm, outside it.
  4. Lag ceilings: \(\PairingLagCeiling = \LatchClearance/\ApproachSpeed\), so 2 mm \(/\) 0.03 m/s \(=\) 66.7 ms and 2 mm \(/\) 0.10 m/s \(=\) 20 ms.

Result: The same lag fits the modeled pairing-error allocation at the guarded approach and exceeds it at the faster one.

Systems insight: A timing error becomes a position error at the speed of the motion, just as observation age becomes blind travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay\) in the stopping budget derived in Kinematic stopping distance and pre-brake delay. The faster approach cannot be collected without the exposure-midpoint alignment of the four-stage pipeline in section 5.5.2.3.

When an imitation learning model trains on misaligned pairs, it fits a relationship in which the action appears to precede the visual evidence that prompted it. During closed-loop deployment, that artificial phase lag appears as overshoot, control hunting, and chattering against rigid surfaces. Because displacement grows linearly with lag, each approach speed has a lag ceiling equal to the clearance divided by the speed, and figure 3 places those ceilings beside a second timing error the notebook did not count. A rolling-shutter sensor exposes its pixel rows one after another rather than all at once, so the bottom row of a frame is captured later than the top (section 5.5.2.2 quantifies the delay). A full-frame rolling-shutter skew of 15 ms to 33 ms is comparable to the uncompensated lag, so a logger that stamps every frame correctly can still breach the faster approach’s 20 ms ceiling through readout alone.

Hardware timestamps vs. software arrival timestamps

The 35 ms pairing lag in notebook 5.1 arises when a logger stamps a sensor packet with the clock reading at the instant a Linux thread wakes up to read it rather than when the hardware captured the sample. Even on the \(1.0\text{ ms}\) kinematic stream, half a millisecond of kernel scheduling jitter cuts a nominal tick in half or stretches it by half, doubling or shrinking the finite-difference velocity label for the exact same physical motion. Both errors come from confusing when an optical or electrical event happened in hardware with when the operating system noticed it in software:

  • Software arrival timestamps (\(\HostArrivalTimestamp\)): In naive logging pipelines, an image frame or CAN packet is timestamped when the Linux kernel handles the device interrupt or when a user-space thread dequeues the buffer from Video4Linux2 (V4L2) or a socket. This host timestamp incorporates camera pixel readout time, USB 3 or MIPI CSI-2 bus serialization, Direct Memory Access (DMA) latency, and operating system scheduling jitter: \[\HostArrivalTimestamp = \ExposureMidpointTime + \frac{\ExposureDuration}{2} + \FrameReadoutTime + \BusSerializationTime + \DriverInterruptLatency + \TimestampJitterDelay.\] Host timestamps lag physical reality by \(20\text{--}45\text{ ms}\). In an untuned system, operating system scheduling jitter (\(\TimestampJitterDelay = \pm 5\text{--}15\text{ ms}\)), driven by kernel preemption latency, CPU C-state power wake-up transitions, and USB host controller interrupt coalescing (where packets are held in hardware buffers until a threshold count or timer expires), corrupts numerical differentiation. When joint velocities are computed via backward difference: \[\GeneralizedVelocities_k = \frac{\GeneralizedCoordinates_k - \GeneralizedCoordinates_{k-1}}{\TimeInstant_k - \TimeInstant_{k-1}},\] a nominal \(1\text{ kHz}\) sampling interval (\(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant = 1.0\text{ ms}\)) perturbed by software timestamp jitter \(\TimestampJitterDelay = \pm 0.5\text{ ms}\) causes the denominator to fluctuate between \(0.5\text{ ms}\) and \(1.5\text{ ms}\). The effect is doubling the inferred velocity at the shorter interval and reducing it by one third at the longer interval, for the same measured displacement. If thread preemption reverses packet dequeuing order (\(\TimeInstant_k < \TimeInstant_{k-1}\)), the computed velocity reverses sign, injecting spurious velocity spikes into the training targets.
  • Hardware PTP timestamps (\(\PtpHardwareTimestamp\)): Hardware timestamps isolate the physical event from host scheduling delays. IEEE 1588 Precision Time Protocol (PTP) estimates clock offset across distributed nodes using a deterministic two-way message exchange.5 The reference clock sends at \(\TimeInstant_1\) and receives the reply at \(\TimeInstant_4\); the remote clock receives at \(\TimeInstant_2\) and replies at \(\TimeInstant_3\). Under symmetric path delays, the propagation delay and offset are: \[\PtpPropagationDelay = \frac{(\TimeInstant_4 - \TimeInstant_1) - (\TimeInstant_3 - \TimeInstant_2)}{2}, \qquad \ClockOffset = \frac{(\TimeInstant_2 - \TimeInstant_1) - (\TimeInstant_4 - \TimeInstant_3)}{2}.\] Because the forward timestamp difference \(\TimeInstant_2 - \TimeInstant_1 = \PtpPropagationDelay + \ClockOffset\) adds the remote clock offset to the one-way propagation delay while the return difference \(\TimeInstant_4 - \TimeInstant_3 = \PtpPropagationDelay - \ClockOffset\) subtracts that same offset, adding the two differences cancels \(\ClockOffset\) to isolate the propagation delay and subtracting them cancels \(\PtpPropagationDelay\) to isolate the clock offset. Hardware timestamping bounds uncertainty, but a network packet timestamp does not automatically equal an optical exposure midpoint. Production logs must preserve the capture event, clock domain identity, and bounded conversion error. The key collection decision is whether that uncertainty fits within the task’s spatial alignment tolerance.

5 Precision Time Protocol (PTP): IEEE 1588 was first published in 2002 for test-and-measurement and industrial-automation networks, and its current revision dates from 2019 (IEEE 2020). It reaches sub-microsecond agreement only when both endpoints timestamp packets in hardware at the network interface. Its weak point is the symmetric-path assumption in the offset formula, because a link whose two directions differ in delay biases the computed offset by half that difference, and every observation-action pairing in the archive inherits the bias.

IEEE. 2020. IEEE Std 1588-2019: IEEE Standard for a Precision Clock Synchronization Protocol for Networked Measurement and Control Systems. Institute of Electrical; Electronics Engineers. https://doi.org/10.1109/IEEESTD.2020.9120376.

↪ Protocol: The IEEE 1588 message exchange, its clock servo, and its path-asymmetry uncertainty are developed in Clock Synchronization and Network Timestamps.

Rolling shutter skew across camera streams

When a camera with a rolling shutter pans past the cage door or rides on a moving link, straight vertical bars bend into diagonal arcs because the bottom pixel row is exposed up to \(33\text{ ms}\) later than the top row. This distortion is rolling shutter skew: low-cost consumer sensors and webcams expose pixel rows one after another rather than all at once. For a sensor with \(\ImageHeight\) vertical scanlines and line readout duration \(\RollingShutterRowReadout\), row \(\SensorRowIndex \in [0, \ImageHeight-1]\) begins its exposure at \(\RowExposureStartTime(\SensorRowIndex)\), and its exposure midpoint, half an exposure later, depends linearly on its vertical coordinate: \[\RowExposureStartTime(\SensorRowIndex) + \frac{\ExposureDuration}{2} = \ExposureStartTime + \SensorRowIndex \, \RollingShutterRowReadout + \frac{\ExposureDuration}{2}.\] Across a full frame, the temporal skew between the top row (\(\SensorRowIndex = 0\)) and bottom row (\(\SensorRowIndex = \ImageHeight - 1\)) accumulates to: \[\RollingShutterFrameSkew = (\ImageHeight - 1) \, \RollingShutterRowReadout, \tag{4}\] which for common sensors lies between about 15 ms and 33 ms, the upper end being one full frame period at 30 Hz. When the robot arm or camera moves at 0.50 m/s, the bottom of the manipulator in the image is captured while the physical link has moved up to 16.5 mm relative to the top of the link in the same frame. Even at the faster latch approach of 0.10 m/s, the worst-case skew moves the gripper 3.3 mm within one frame, exceeding the 2 mm clearance. This geometric warping (the “jello effect”) deforms straight edges into curved arcs and breaks rigid-body geometry.

Independent rolling shutters can depict the same moving arm at different poses within nominally simultaneous frames. Hardware-triggered global shutters simplify alignment; rolling shutters require a row-time model and a motion bound or correction. Sensor choice must follow the task tolerance rather than an unyielding demand for a single shutter architecture.

↪ The same row timing as a spatial error on a moving base, charged against clearance and removed by per-row motion compensation, is developed in Capture Physics and Calibration.

A four-stage alignment pipeline

Hardware timestamps fix when each sample was taken, and the row-time model fixes when each part of an image was exposed. A collection pipeline still has to turn those facts into training pairs whose pairing error fits inside the clearance budget of notebook 5.1. It can implement that alignment contract in four stages:

  1. Hardware-triggered optical exposure midpoint: The real-time MCU dispatches hardware GPIO sync pulses or PTP-synchronized trigger packets to every triggerable camera. The rig mixes shutter types. The wrist camera’s global shutter exposes all rows together, while the navigation camera and the external cameras’ color sensors read out row by row, so their frames also need the row-time model above to assign each row its own midpoint. The camera latches the trigger at \(\ExposureStartTime\), and the driver calculates the true optical midpoint \(\ExposureMidpointTime = \ExposureStartTime + \ExposureDuration / 2\) in hardware PTP clock units (\(\PtpHardwareTimestamp\)), avoiding host arrival time as the exposure timestamp.6
  2. Lock-free time-sorted jitter compensation: Because network and bus transfers experience variable transport delay \(\TimestampJitterDelay\), incoming sensor packets are stamped with their hardware PTP capture timestamp and inserted into a lock-free, time-sorted jitter ring buffer in shared memory. An asynchronous consumer thread advances a playhead lagging real-time by a fixed, bounded window (\(\JitterBufferPlayoutDelay = 15\text{--}25\text{ ms}\)), absorbing transport bursts and re-ordering packets into monotonic hardware timestamp sequence.
  3. Cubic Hermite kinematic interpolation: Because joint encoders sample at \(1\text{ kHz}\) (\(1\text{ ms}\) period) while cameras capture at \(30\text{ Hz}\) or \(60\text{ Hz}\), the camera midpoint \(\ExposureMidpointTime\) falls between two discrete MCU telemetry ticks \(\TimeInstant_k\) and \(\TimeInstant_{k+1}\). The synchronization thread interpolates joint angles \(\GeneralizedCoordinates(\ExposureMidpointTime)\) using cubic Hermite splines across the bounding kinematic records \((\TimeInstant_k, \GeneralizedCoordinates_k, \GeneralizedVelocities_k)\) and \((\TimeInstant_{k+1}, \GeneralizedCoordinates_{k+1}, \GeneralizedVelocities_{k+1})\): \[\GeneralizedCoordinates(\ExposureMidpointTime) = \HermiteBasisFunction_{00}(\HermiteInterpolationFraction) \GeneralizedCoordinates_k + \HermiteBasisFunction_{10}(\HermiteInterpolationFraction) \TelemetryTickInterval \GeneralizedVelocities_k + \HermiteBasisFunction_{01}(\HermiteInterpolationFraction) \GeneralizedCoordinates_{k+1} + \HermiteBasisFunction_{11}(\HermiteInterpolationFraction) \TelemetryTickInterval \GeneralizedVelocities_{k+1},\] where \(\TelemetryTickInterval = \TimeInstant_{k+1} - \TimeInstant_k = 1\text{ ms}\), \(\HermiteInterpolationFraction = (\ExposureMidpointTime - \TimeInstant_k)/\TelemetryTickInterval \in [0, 1]\) is the fraction of the tick elapsed at the exposure midpoint, and \(\HermiteBasisFunction_{ij}(\HermiteInterpolationFraction)\) are the cubic Hermite basis functions (Smooth Trajectories and Bumpless Transfer). This gives a smooth state estimate between valid samples. The interpolation error still needs a bound, especially across contact or missing telemetry.
  4. Pre-serialization alignment: Container formats store multi-rate topics with the timestamps provided by the logger. Alignment may occur during capture or in a versioned preprocessing pass. Preserve the raw timestamps and interpolation provenance in either case, so the training pair carries an estimated state and its uncertainty rather than an assertion of exact simultaneity.

6 Hardware Exposure Triggering: A dedicated GPIO or TTL pulse from the real-time microcontroller starts every camera’s exposure on the same electrical edge, instead of a software trigger issued through the host’s driver stack. Triggered global-shutter cameras then begin exposing within microseconds of one another, which leaves the exposure duration, not the trigger path, as the dominant term in the midpoint timestamp.

The same pairing contract governs deployment. A policy trained on aligned pairs must also be fed aligned observations and have its actions executed when the training data implies. UMI measures each observation stream’s latency and aligns all streams to the slowest, usually the camera. It then sends commands ahead of time to cover the arm’s and gripper’s execution latency, and it discards actions whose execution time has already passed. With latency matching disabled, the same policy’s motion became visibly jittery (Chi et al. 2024). UMI also expresses actions relative to the gripper’s current pose rather than in a fixed robot frame, which removes the dependence on a base calibration and lets one policy run on more than one arm.

Checkpoint 5.1: Action labels and pairing time

A training pair is only as trustworthy as the tap it imitates and the time it assigns to each observation.

A tap and a timestamp are only as good as the loop that produced them. In teleoperation that loop runs through a human, and the interface between human and machine shapes the recorded dynamics, storage bandwidth, and hardware wear long before the data reaches a model (section 5.6).

5.6 Teleoperation Demonstration Costs

When an operator guides a leader arm to unlatch the warehouse cage door, every corrective nudge must traverse a multi-stage physical loop before the robot can respond. Teleoperation is a data-generation pipeline with a human embedded in the real-time feedback loop. It charges for each demonstration in three currencies: the phase lag and stability margin of the loop itself, the write bandwidth and storage the session demands, and the cell time and component life a campaign consumes. The loop comes first (figure 4). The operator sees the scene on a display after a delay \(\DisplayPipelineLatency\) and perceives, decides, and moves the leader in \(\OperatorResponseLatency\). The command crosses to the follower in \(\TeleoperationCommandLatency\). The follower’s actuators then accelerate the links against inertia, drivetrain friction, and contact in \(\FollowerMechanicalLatency\), before the camera shows the result on the next cycle.

↪ Where a deployment moves operators’ labor, and which uses of their recorded sessions it must bar, is developed in The Labor the Machine Moves.

By mapping each leader joint directly to the corresponding follower joint (\(\LeaderJointPositions \equiv \FollowerJointPositions\)), this isomorphic leader-follower topology sidesteps numerical inverse kinematics and algorithmic kinematic singularities. Even without software kinematic conversion, however, every recorded trajectory remains bound to the physical transport and neuromuscular delays of the complete loop, placing hard upper bounds on corrective bandwidth.

5.6.1 Human-in-the-loop latency and bilateral passivity breakdown

The operator’s own reaction is 58.8 percent of the 340 ms correction loop.

Suppose the follower gripper grazes the side of the latch strike plate at time \(\TimeInstant\). If a logger naively pairs the observation \(\MultimodalObservation_{t}\) captured at that instant with the command the operator issued at \(\TimeInstant\), it pairs a contact state with an action chosen for a trajectory state 250 ms earlier, before the contact ever happened. A behavioral cloning policy trained on those pairs learns that delay as intentional strategy and, when run autonomously at high control frequencies, over-corrects into destructive oscillations. That stale pairing arises because every stage of the teleoperation loop adds phase delay. In an illustrative reference setup with a high-speed camera and a wired local network, display pipeline processing takes \(\DisplayPipelineLatency =\) 50 ms across sensor exposure, H.264 decoding, and display raster refresh. Human visual perception, motor planning, and neuromuscular transmission add an operator response latency of \(\OperatorResponseLatency =\) 200 ms. Command quantization, packetization, and fieldbus transport add \(\TeleoperationCommandLatency =\) 10 ms. Actuator torque ramp-up against link inertia then adds a mechanical response latency of \(\FollowerMechanicalLatency =\) 80 ms before the arm reaches the commanded acceleration. Their sum is the teleoperation round-trip latency \(\TeleoperationRoundTripLatency\), the elapsed time from a physical contact disturbance to the corrective actuator response: \[\TeleoperationRoundTripLatency = \DisplayPipelineLatency + \OperatorResponseLatency + \TeleoperationCommandLatency + \FollowerMechanicalLatency. \tag{5}\]

Following the contact at time \(\TimeInstant\) through those four stages shows why the recorded command lags the state. The operator sees the error on the display 50 ms later and commands a physical correction at \(\TimeInstant +{}\) 250 ms. The follower arm finally pushes back at \(\TimeInstant +{}\) 340 ms, after drifting under momentum and contact compliance throughout the interval. Round-trip latency is therefore a property of the resulting dataset rather than just an artifact of the rig, because it injects a systematic phase lag into every recorded demonstration, dominated by the operator’s own reaction time (58.8 percent of the loop in this setup).

This delay differs in kind from the sensor-pipeline delay of section 5.5.2. That one was a pairing artifact of the logger, and exposure-midpoint stamping lets the alignment pipeline account for it. The operator’s delay is a real property of the demonstrator, so no timestamp correction can remove it, and silently erasing it would misstate what the demonstration contains. The logger therefore keeps both times of every pair (the observation’s exposure midpoint and the command’s issue time) and records the session’s measured loop latency. Training can then treat the observation-to-action interval as a declared part of the demonstration, either by conditioning the policy on enough observation history to span it or by pairing each command with the earlier frame the operator was answering. Deployment can then match the interval it was trained with, as UMI’s latency matching does (section 5.5.2.3). Because the operator’s reaction varies from command to command, no single fixed shift recovers the exact frame each command answered, which is why the latch campaign records the interval instead of baking one correction into the archive.

Force reflection introduces a deeper stability challenge into the collection interface. In bilateral teleoperation, the operator manipulates the leader device while measured contact forces from the follower arm reflect back across a delayed communication channel. Over a closed motion cycle of period \(\MotionCyclePeriod\), the net mechanical energy delivered at that port is: \[\PortCycleNetEnergy = \int_0^{\MotionCyclePeriod} \ReflectedContactForce(\TimeInstant - \PureTransportDelay) \PortVelocity(\TimeInstant)\,d\TimeInstant.\] The sign of \(\PortCycleNetEnergy\) depends on the force–velocity phase relationship. Under the declared port orientation, energy delivered in excess of received and initially stored energy violates passivity;7 a positive value over one cycle alone does not establish that excess. In an undelayed elastic contact, the reflected force opposes the leader handle during compression and drops as the handle rebounds, whereas transmission delay \(\PureTransportDelay\) causes peak contact force to continue pushing the leader handle after the operator has already reversed direction, aligning force with rebound velocity \(\PortVelocity(\TimeInstant)\) to pump net mechanical energy into each oscillation cycle and excite contact instability (Anderson and Spong 1989). The wave-variable transformation derived in Passivity and Energy-Bounded Interaction keeps the ideal delayed channel passive and lossless under its constant-delay model, while passivity observers (monitors that track the energy a port has generated and dissipate any excess) restore passivity by injecting variable damping. Preserving channel passivity imposes a force-fidelity cost at contact. Injected damping, and the explicit low-pass filtering often added to delayed force channels, suppress the fastest components of the reflected force first, so a stabilized channel behaves as a low-pass filter on contact. In data collection, this imposes an unavoidable label-fidelity trade-off. The filtering that stabilizes delayed haptic feedback also attenuates the sharp contact transients the operator needs to demonstrate precise tactile insertion. System builders must log the active feedback architecture and filter cutoffs alongside raw trajectories to document this phase distortion.

7 Passivity: A passive system cannot deliver more energy through its ports than it has received or initially stored. A well-posed, power-conserving interconnection of passive components preserves their combined passivity inequality. Stronger stability conclusions require bounds on stored energy and appropriate strictness or detectability conditions, as developed in Passivity and Energy-Bounded Interaction. Anderson and Spong (1989) showed that a delayed communication channel need not be passive and restored passivity for any constant delay with a scattering transformation, and Niemeyer and Slotine (1991) recast the idea as wave variables. The price is paid in force fidelity at contact, and the demonstrations record that price.

Anderson, Robert J., and Mark W. Spong. 1989. “Bilateral Control of Teleoperators with Time Delay.” IEEE Transactions on Automatic Control 34 (5): 494–501. https://doi.org/10.1109/9.24201.
Niemeyer, Günter, and Jean-Jacques E. Slotine. 1991. “Stable Adaptive Teleoperation.” IEEE Journal of Oceanic Engineering 16 (1): 152–62. https://doi.org/10.1109/48.64895.

The mechanical interface therefore filters which behaviors can reach the archive at all. A force-reflecting leader allows an operator to feel mechanical binding and ease off insertion forces. A visual-only Cartesian rate controller instead forces the step-and-wait hunting of section 5.4.1, because the operator must wait for display frames to confirm each small motion. A backdriven joint-space leader such as ALOHA, recorded at \(50\text{ Hz}\) (Zhao et al. 2023), occupies an intermediate design point, providing inertial and friction feedback from the leader mechanism itself without reflecting follower contact forces. Across all these interfaces, human operators instinctively decelerate prior to contact, because closing a high-gain corrective loop around round-trip latency \(\TeleoperationRoundTripLatency\) would erode phase margin to zero. Table 2 categorizes the dominant teleoperation architectures and the specific data collection artifacts they produce.

5.6.2 Ingestion rate and the preservation requirement

The loop’s latency is the first cost a demonstration carries; the second is what it takes to write everything the loop produced to disk without silent data loss. If an onboard logger tries to stream raw multi-camera video and \(1\text{ kHz}\) kinematics onto a SATA solid-state drive, write buffers overflow within seconds, dropping either the camera frames or the four action taps of section 5.5.1 that explain what the permission path did. Whether an edge collection computer can sustain a teleoperated session without dropping samples is storage-bus arithmetic: the aggregate write rate set against the sequential write bandwidth of the drive.

Napkin Math 5.2: The cheapest stream
Problem: One teleoperated door-opening session on the warehouse mobile manipulator logs every camera and every axis raw. What sustained write rate and storage does it demand, and what share of the bytes carries the four action taps?

Variables (camera geometry is datasheet; logging formats and shift length are chosen):

  • Navigation camera: 4056 by 3040 px at 60 Hz, logged as 24-bit RGB (3 bytes/px).
  • Wrist camera: 1440 by 1080 px at 60 Hz, logged the same way.
  • External cameras: 2 RGB-D cameras viewing the door, color at 1920 by 1080 px and 30 Hz, with depth aligned to the color frame as raw uint16 millimeters (2 bytes/px).
  • Kinematics: 9 axes (two base wheels, seven arm joints) logged at 1 kHz. Each step stores, for every axis, 6 values of 4 bytes (position and velocity, which form the measured response \(\MeasuredResponseTap\); measured torque; and the requested, mapped, and enforced commands), plus one 8-byte timestamp.
  • Shift: 8 hours of collection on one machine.

Math:

  1. Kinematic stream: 9 \(\times\) 6 \(\times\) 4 B \(+\) 8 B \(=\) 224 B per step, and at 1 kHz that is 0.22 MB/s.
  2. Navigation camera: 4056 \(\times\) 3040 px \(\times\) 3 B \(\times\) 60 Hz \(=\) 2219.44 MB/s. The wrist camera’s product is 279.94 MB/s.
  3. External pair: 2 \(\times\) 1920 \(\times\) 1080 px \(\times\) (3 B \(+\) 2 B) \(\times\) 30 Hz \(=\) 622.08 MB/s, of which 373.25 MB/s is color and 248.83 MB/s depth.
  4. Total and storage: The streams sum to 3121.68 MB/s, about 3.12 GB/s; over one hour that is 11.24 TB, and over the shift 89.9 TB.

Result: Raw logging needs a sustained 3.12 GB/s write path, and the kinematic stream is 0.007 percent of the bytes.

Systems insight: The navigation camera alone writes 4.0× what a SATA SSD sustains (550 MB/s). The full session fits within a Gen4 NVMe drive’s 7 GB/s, but only by consuming storage at 11.24 TB per hour. Intra-frame video compression at an illustrative 8× brings the rate to about 390.2 MB/s (1.40 TB per hour) at the cost of encoder latency and artifacts in the frames the policy will train on. The kinematic stream carries all four action taps, so the record that preserves the causal chain is the cheapest stream to log, and a logger that drops data under buffer pressure must never drop it first.

The navigation camera alone writes 4.0× what a SATA SSD sustains; only the compressed session fits under it.

Keeping that write rate under the drive ceiling with video compression trades storage bandwidth against visual fidelity, because lossy encoders discard fine spatial detail at the latch edge and inject blocking artifacts into the frames the policy trains on. A session can be stored in custom HDF5 files, ROS 2 MCAP bags (a container format for multimodal robotics data), or standardized schemas such as LeRobot (Cadene et al. 2024). Whichever format is used, the logger must flush complete chunks to non-volatile storage before a power drop or process halt can truncate the write buffer. It must also carry all four control taps, hardware timestamps, clock-domain identifiers, missing-sample flags, and spatial calibration parameters through every format conversion. Otherwise, a file that deserializes without error proves only that its byte layout is intact, not that its sensory and motor streams remain physically synchronized.

5.6.3 Operational yield and mechanical fatigue budgeting

In this illustrative session, setup, rest, resets, and rejected attempts leave under a quarter of the scheduled hour as retained demonstrations.

Sizing the NVMe drive settles what one hour in the workcell writes to disk, but less than a quarter of that scheduled hour survives as usable demonstration motion. Before the arm records a single latch opening, a scheduled hour in the workcell pays for bus synchronization, camera and torque-sensor calibration, and mandatory operator rest. The active motion window that remains is further divided among door-reset strokes, mid-trial contact trips, and offline quality filtering. Because every attempt, whether retained or discarded, flexes the wrist cable harness and reverses load on the harmonic drive, a collection campaign is bounded as much by component fatigue life as by facility reservations.
