The ultimate metric of collection efficiency is demonstration yield: the number of validated, policy-ready trajectory records produced in a scheduled session after accounting for cell startup, sensor calibration, operator rest, mechanical resets, and offline quality filtering. Given an active motion window \(\ActiveMotionDuration\), an attempt-and-reset cycle duration \(\AttemptCycleDuration = \AttemptDuration + \ResetDuration\), and an end-to-end retention rate \(\DemonstrationRetentionRate\), the session yields: \[\UsableDemonstrationYield = \left\lfloor \frac{\ActiveMotionDuration}{\AttemptCycleDuration} \right\rfloor \DemonstrationRetentionRate. \tag{6}\] Mechanical fatigue accumulates per physical motion cycle rather than per facility hour. This illustrative accounting assigns the same stress-cycle counts to failed and successful attempts and assumes component ratings expressed under the \(\RatedFatigueLife\) convention. Under it, the rating is the cumulative cycle count at which 10 percent of an identical component population is statistically expected to fail.8 Notebook 5.3 evaluates an illustrative door-opening collection session and its full campaign in both currencies: facility hours and component fatigue cycles.

8 \(\RatedFatigueLife\) Rated Life: The convention comes from rolling-bearing fatigue theory, where Lundberg and Palmgren (1949) modeled the scatter of bearing life as a probability of survival and rated a bearing by the life that 90 percent of a population reaches. A rating chosen that way is a population statistic, not a guarantee for one part. A fleet of ten collection arms, each run to its cable’s rated life, should therefore expect about one cable to fail before the budget says it will.

Lundberg, Gustaf, and Arvid Palmgren. 1949. “Dynamic Capacity of Rolling Bearings.” Journal of Applied Mechanics 16 (2): 165–72. https://doi.org/10.1115/1.4009930.

Napkin Math 5.3: The flex-cable ceiling
Problem: The latch campaign must retain 5,000 demonstrations. How much scheduled cell time does that cost, and which wrist component’s rated life does it spend fastest?

Variables (illustrative except the chosen session length and target):

  • Session \(\ScheduledSessionDuration =\) 60 min (chosen), leaving an active window \(\ActiveMotionDuration =\) 35 min after startup, calibration, and operator rest
  • Attempt \(\AttemptDuration =\) 25 s, one teleoperated opening; reset \(\ResetDuration =\) 15 s, the door re-latched by its fixture
  • In-trial failure rate 20 percent; post-collection quality filtering 15 percent
  • Wear per attempt: \(\GearTorqueReversalCount =\) 14 torque reversals of the wrist’s strain-wave gear; \(\CableFlexCycleCount =\) 8 flex cycles of its internal cable bundle (6 while opening, 2 on the return stroke)
  • Rated lives \(\GearRatedFatigueLife =\) 5,000,000 stress cycles and \(\CableRatedFatigueLife =\) 200,000 bending cycles

Math:

  1. Session: \(\AttemptCycleDuration =\) 25 s \(+\) 15 s \(=\) 40 s, and \(\lfloor\) 35 min \(/\) 40 s \(\rfloor =\) 52 attempts, with 20 s left idle.
  2. Yield (equation 6): \(\DemonstrationRetentionRate = (1 -\) 0.20\()(1 -\) 0.15\() =\) 0.68, so \(\UsableDemonstrationYield =\) 52 \(\times\) 0.68 \(=\) 35.36 episodes, 14.73 min of validated motion, 24.6 percent of the scheduled hour.
  3. Campaign: \(\lceil\) 5,000 \(/\) 0.68 \(\rceil =\) 7,353 attempts at 52 per session, or 141.4 h of scheduled cell time, 4.07× the 5,000 \(\times\) 25 s of motion the campaign keeps.
  4. Wear: 7,353 \(\times\) 14 \(=\) 102,942 gear cycles, 2.1 percent of \(\GearRatedFatigueLife\); 7,353 \(\times\) 8 \(=\) 58,824 cable bends, 29.4 percent of \(\CableRatedFatigueLife\).

Result: Less than a quarter of each scheduled hour survives as retained demonstration time, and one campaign spends far more of the cable’s rated life than of the gear’s.

Systems insight: The internal flex cable consumes the larger fraction of its assumed rating, informing maintenance planning rather than setting an individual replacement schedule. Three campaigns on one machine would consume most of its rated life, and an intermittent conductor break corrupts the very sensor streams being recorded. Because every attempt costs wear whether or not it is retained, the retention rate \(\DemonstrationRetentionRate\) divides both bills, while a faster reset shortens only the cell time.

Operator rest requirements and fatigue rates depend heavily on interface ergonomics and cognitive task difficulty; teams must empirically calibrate sustained operator throughput and retention on their specific hardware. A collection campaign must therefore be budgeted in both validated trajectory time and consumed fatigue life, not in facility clock hours. Table 3 breaks down the time and mechanical wear budgets of the illustrative session stage by stage.

↪ Per-cycle wear accounting for trial-based learning on the machine is developed in Reinforcement Learning and Environment Interaction.

Public collection shows what that yield arithmetic produces at community scale. The open-source LeRobot toolchain records teleoperated sessions in a common format and publishes them to the Hugging Face Hub with an auto-generated metadata card (Cadene et al. 2024). Figure 5 plots, from the Hub’s own listing, how many such datasets exist and how many episodes each holds.

Cadene, Rémi, Simon Alibert, Alexander Toshev, Thomas Sen, Quentin Gallouédec, Adil Zouitine, et al. 2024. LeRobot: State-of-the-Art Machine Learning for Real-World Robotics in PyTorch.

The Hub held 1,085 such datasets at the end of 2024, 26,548 a year later, and 80,132 by the retrieval date. Episode counts tell a different story. Of the 59,031 datasets whose card records one, 58 percent hold fewer than 16 episodes, and only 2 percent hold 512 or more. The median dataset falls in the 8 to 15 episode bin, less than the roughly 35 validated demonstrations of the illustrative hour in table 3. Community data therefore grows wide rather than deep. The archive gains embodiments, scenes, and operators far faster than it gains repeated coverage of any one machine’s states, and pooling it inherits the transfer problem of section 5.9.1.

The three costs compound into a fourth that no bill shows. Teleoperated demonstrations are bounded by what a human operator can perceive through video displays, coordinate through mechanical input devices, and stabilize against closed-loop delays. A campaign that pays for every attempt in cell time and cable life spends its budget where the operator is comfortable. The resulting dataset reflects the behavioral envelope of the coupled human-machine composite rather than the robot’s full physical dynamics. That selection bias decides which situations the archive covers, and measuring it requires a standard outside the archive (section 5.7).

5.7 Collection Policy Coverage

Every logged latch record carries an ingestion cost, an attributed action label, and a hardware timestamp, yet those fields say nothing about which physical situations the archive covers. In an illustrative contact task where a gripper aligns with a workpiece edge across contact angles \(\GripperContactAngle \in [-15.0^\circ, +15.0^\circ]\) and normal forces up to \(20.0\text{ N}\), binning at \(1.0^\circ\) and \(1.0\text{ N}\) forms an operational grid of 600 discrete cells. Suppose two collection policies each record \(1{,}000{,}000\) transitions at \(100\text{ Hz}\) with identical sensors. A cautious teleoperator keeps contact within \(\pm 2.0^\circ\) of perpendicular under \(4.0\text{ to }6.0\text{ N}\) of normal force, populating 8 cells and leaving 98.7 percent of the grid empty. An exploratory policy approaches across the full angular range, slips, and loads the contact between \(1.0\text{ and }18.0\text{ N}\), filling 510 cells and leaving 15.0 percent empty. Although both datasets have identical record counts, storage sizes, and hardware timestamps, they answer different operational questions. Record count therefore fails as a measure twice over: correlated control steps hold few independent samples (equation 2), and even independent samples support only the cells where they land.

That disparity arises because a physical dataset comes from running a closed-loop policy on hardware and logging the resulting transitions, violating the independent-and-identically-distributed assumption of standard empirical risk minimization (section 5.2). Instead, it traces an empirical state-action occupancy distribution (Spencer et al. 2021) \(\CollectionStateVisitationDistribution(\DecisionProcessState, \DemonstrationActionLabel)\) generated by a specific collecting policy \(\pi_{\text{col}}\) under its own control biases, interface delays, and risk tolerance. Formally, imitation learning minimizes prediction error weighted solely by this empirical occupancy: \[\BehaviorCloningLoss(\ModelParameters) = \mathbb{E}_{(\DecisionProcessState, \DemonstrationActionLabel) \sim \CollectionStateVisitationDistribution} \left[ \ell(\Policy(\DecisionProcessState), \DemonstrationActionLabel) \right].\] The learned policy \(\Policy\) therefore receives supervisory gradient updates only over the empirical support hull \(\text{supp}(\CollectionStateVisitationDistribution) = \{ \DecisionProcessState \in \WorldStateSpace \mid \CollectionStateVisitationDistribution(\DecisionProcessState) > 0 \}\). Outside this narrow support hull, the network’s outputs are unconstrained by training demonstrations.

Supervisory labels exist only where the collecting policy visited and commanded an actuator action. In unvisited states, the dataset contains neither an optimal control response nor proof that a safe trajectory exists. Supervised loss functions cannot distinguish an inherently unstable physical configuration from a benign unvisited state or an outright collision hazard. Whether an autonomous policy generalizes in such regions, fails silently, or triggers a safety stop cannot be deduced from the archive alone; resolving that uncertainty requires targeted recovery demonstrations, expert relabeling, or explicit run-time gating. Consider a policy trained exclusively on the cautious teleoperation data. If unexpected surface friction perturbs the gripper to contact angle \(\GripperContactAngle = +4.5^\circ\), outside the eight demonstrated cells, the policy network evaluates an input unconstrained by demonstrations. One control step later, its ungrounded action drives the contact to \(+6.0^\circ\) at \(11.0\text{ N}\), another state lacking corrective labels in the training corpus. Compounding errors push the manipulator progressively farther from the nominal corridor until the gripper jams against the fixture. This cascade illustrates endogenous covariate shift (definition 5.1) triggered by a coverage gap.

5.7.1 Choosing a tractable coverage table

The contact-angle example uses a compact grid because it isolates the parameters relevant to a single mating feature. Extending that dense discretization across all robot state variables triggers the curse of dimensionality in continuous physical interaction.9 Consider a mobile manipulator with a 7-degree-of-freedom (7-DoF) arm, a 2-DoF differential-drive base, a 6-axis force-torque sensor at the wrist, and a 6-DoF target workpiece pose. The continuous state space has dimension: \[\StateSpaceDimension = 7\text{ (joint angles)} + 7\text{ (joint velocities)} + 2\text{ (wheel speeds)} + 6\text{ (wrench components)} + 6\text{ (workpiece pose)} = 28.\]

9 Curse of Dimensionality: Bellman coined the phrase for dynamic programming, whose value tables grow exponentially with the number of state variables (Bellman 1957). A raw physical coverage table meets the same combinatorial barrier. Dynamic programming scaled by approximating value functions over compact feature spaces; a physical coverage table stays tractable the same way, by listing the few task parameters that determine success rather than the dense continuous state.

Bellman, Richard. 1957. Dynamic Programming. Princeton University Press.

If an engineering team partitions each dimension into a crude \(\DiscretizationBinCount = 10\) bins (\(36^\circ\) per angle bin and \(10\text{ N}\) per wrench bin, far too coarse to guide contact tasks whose tolerances are a few millimeters), the discretized state space contains \(\DiscretizationBinCount^{\StateSpaceDimension} = 10^{28}\) cells. A fleet of 1,000 robots logging at \(1\text{ kHz}\) since the universe began, about 13.8 billion years ago, would hold 4.35 × 10²³ records, enough to visit at most 0.004 percent of those cells even if every record landed in a different one. At a resolution fine enough to guide contact (\(\DiscretizationBinCount = 100\) bins per axis), the count grows to \(10^{56}\) cells. Brute-force observation cannot cover a continuous physical state space; a coverage table must describe the task with a few parameters instead.

A fleet logging since the universe began could visit at most 0.004 percent of even the coarse grid.

Evaluating dataset completeness against the empirical extremes of collected values is fundamentally circular. A collection logger that records forces exclusively between \(4.0\text{ N}\) and \(6.0\text{ N}\) trivially reports full coverage of that observed interval. Coverage must instead be evaluated against the operational conditions that deployment demands, formulated before any demonstration data is collected.

Definition 5.3: Operational design domain

Operational design domain (ODD) is the declared set of conditions (object poses, surface conditions, payloads, speeds, and disturbances) under which a machine is intended to operate, stated independently of any data collected within it.

  1. Significance: The ODD serves as the denominator for every coverage claim. For example, the latch archive, as the join later in this section shows, populates only a single cell of the scenario table its door mechanism requires, a fraction that no volume of redundant nominal episodes can increase. Subsequent verification, evaluation, and release records can assert operational authority only over explicitly supported regions of the ODD.
  2. Distinction: Deployment requirements define the ODD, whereas the collecting policy defines empirical occupancy. Coverage measured against observed telemetry extremes merely restates empirical occupancy in ODD terminology, producing an illusion of completeness. A scenario table partitions the continuous ODD into structured discrete cells so that deployment requirements and empirical occupancy can be joined.
  3. Common pitfall: Defining the ODD post hoc based on conditions where the machine has already operated successfully rather than where it is required to function. Letting the dataset define its own requirements masks known unvisited failure modes.

In high-dimensional continuous systems, a scenario table avoids voxelizing raw camera pixel streams or continuous joint spaces, which would trigger an intractable combinatorial explosion. Instead, it parameterizes low-dimensional task invariants and environmental boundaries, such as workpiece pose offsets (\(\pm 5\text{ mm}\)), surface friction coefficients, approach speeds, lighting variations (\(100\text{--}1000\text{ lux}\)), and payload masses (\(0\text{--}2\text{ kg}\)). These parameters ground verification in concrete physical operating constraints. While this relational join identifies where the dataset holds empirical experience, populating a cell does not demonstrate that a model trained on those samples executes successfully. A populated cell provides supervisory targets, but verifying autonomous competence still requires closed-loop policy evaluation.

The missing recovery actions identified in figure 1 demonstrate why even a tractable scenario table must list off-nominal perturbations as cells of their own. Repeatedly executing a flawless motion samples progress along the nominal path, whereas corrective demonstrations supply actions that return the machine after an unexpected displacement. A sound scenario table distinguishes recovery experiences from nominal runs rather than treating repeated nominal cycles as coverage of the adjacent state space.

Unsupported operational regimes are exposed by a support envelope relational join, which cross-references empirical occupancy \(\CollectionStateVisitationDistribution(\DecisionProcessState,\DemonstrationActionLabel)\) (where the machine went) against the scenario table \(\RequiredScenarioSet\) (where the machine must be able to go). The table declares a count unit (transitions, independent episodes, or retained demonstrations), an eligibility and retention rule, and a minimum in that same unit. For each cell, the join tallies \(\ScenarioCellRecordCount\) under this policy and partitions the table into cells meeting the minimum (\(\ScenarioCellRecordCount \ge \MinimumCellSupportCount\)), low-count cells, and unvisited blind spots (\(\ScenarioCellRecordCount = 0\)). A populated cell has a nonzero count; it need not meet the minimum. The contact-angle example counts transitions, while the latch campaign’s table counts retained demonstrations. These bins define collection categories. The threshold \(\MinimumCellSupportCount\) represents a declared data requirement rather than a test of policy competence; neither an occupied cell nor a large sample count confers autonomous deployment authority.

A held-out validation split drawn from the same collecting policy can evaluate action prediction accuracy over that occupancy while remaining completely blind to gaps in the required operational domain. The comparison in table 4 separates collection coverage metrics from downstream policy outcomes. Its loss row is the root-mean-square error, in radians, of the predicted action on held-out records, and the cautious archive posts the lower value precisely because a narrow, repetitive archive is easier to fit. Low validation loss does not demonstrate recovery capability in unvisited conditions, just as wide exploratory coverage does not prove that collected corrective labels are dynamically stabilizing. Closed-loop evaluation must test the candidate policy directly against the declared task population.

The cage-door latch campaign illustrates this relational join for the running machine. Its six-cell example records occupancy, without supplying per-cell counts or a minimum-count requirement. Every retained episode approaches the strike plate at the guarded speed of 0.03 m/s with the plate aligned within ±0.5 mm of nominal, and every successful demonstration stays below the illustrative 15 N detection threshold. This observation does not itself qualify the collection response. Evaluated against an uncritical table containing only the nominal plate position at the guarded speed, the archive appears exhaustive. Against the scenario table the deployment actually demands, three plate positions (nominal and ±3 mm) crossed with two approach speeds (0.03 m/s and 0.10 m/s), the identical dataset populates only one cell of six. Every off-nominal plate displacement and every fast approach at 0.10 m/s contains zero demonstrations. The raw logged telemetry does not change between these two assessments; the difference lies entirely in the standard of audit applied. The five unvisited cells therefore travel with the dataset into training as documented operational absences, establishing that the current demonstrations provide no empirical foundation for competence claims in those regimes. Targeted collection can acquire the missing recovery trajectories, but downstream training and closed-loop testing must verify what the resulting policy achieves. Figure 6 sets the two audits side by side and adds a third, the stipulated recovery collection latch-recovery-02 of section 5.8, which fills one more cell and still leaves four known absences.

Checkpoint 5.2: Empirical occupancy and scenario table joins

Before evaluating policy coverage across an operational design domain, audit dataset occupancy and scenario-table counts against these criteria:

Logging additional nominal demonstrations along an unperturbed trajectory cannot eliminate coverage blind spots. Exposing the policy to recovery dynamics requires forcing the system toward its operational boundaries, either through deliberate demonstrator noise injection or through expert supervisory interventions (Kelly et al. 2019; Laskey et al. 2017). Before collecting either, a team must know why each empty cell is empty, and recorded interventions add a harder question, because they mix the learner’s commands and the expert’s in one trace (section 5.8).

5.8 Intervention and Recovery Data

A scenario cell, such as the cage-door latch displaced by \(+3\text{ mm}\), may hold zero demonstrations in the training archive. That zero count alone cannot tell an engineer whether the robot never drifted there, whether the 15 N contact tripwire aborted every attempt that reached it, whether a safety supervisor took over beforehand, or whether an ingestion script threw the failed episodes away. Treating those four absences as interchangeable either trains the policy to imitate a safety abort or discards the exact boundary states needed for recovery. An absent state in an empirical log arises from one of four physical mechanisms during collection:

  1. Unvisited nominal path: The collecting policy never visited the state because nominal rollouts remained entirely within the primary task corridor. This absence represents unmonitored nominal operation, verifiable by cross-referencing trajectory logs against the scenario table.
  2. Permission-path interception: An active safety barrier or deterministic reflex in the permission path detected an impending boundary violation and truncated the motion before reaching the state, as the 15 N contact tripwire does during a misaligned latch attempt. The permission path logs record this barrier trip directly.
  3. Human supervisory takeover: A human safety operator perceived a developing hazard and disengaged the autonomous policy before contact occurred. The permission path’s log flags this disengagement explicitly.
  4. Post-collection censoring: The machine entered the hazardous state and collided with an obstacle or jammed a joint, but an automated ingestion script discarded the episode as corrupt or incomplete. Dataset inspection cannot detect this absence because the failure transitions were purged before the training arrays were serialized to disk.

Each mechanism produces an identical zero count in the scenario table, yet each calls for a different response. An unvisited cell calls for targeted collection. An intercepted state belongs to the permission path, which already refuses it, and is not a target for the policy to learn. A takeover leaves learner-visited states that curation can turn into corrective supervision. A censored episode is a pipeline defect, recoverable only if the raw recording still exists. Conflating them leads to wrong conclusions about closed-loop policy stability, and the third mechanism needs the most care.

Human takeovers and automated disengagements introduce a selection trap into fleet data. An intervention occurs when the autonomous policy enters a region of state space it handles poorly and its tracking error or uncertainty crosses a threshold. A trajectory recorded after takeover therefore shows an expert recovering from an edge-case disturbance, not nominal task execution. The distribution of states visited during these rescues, \(\TakeoverStateVisitationDistribution\), is conditioned on policy failure. Treating intervention logs as if they were drawn from the nominal distribution \(\ExpertStateVisitationDistribution\) distorts the training distribution toward states that exist only because the prior policy failed.

Appending a rescued episode directly to a behavioral cloning dataset can make unsafe policy commands look like expert demonstrations. Before takeover, the learned policy may issue commands that compound heading error or contact force; after takeover, the expert issues corrective commands from states the learner visited. Those drift states are valuable for corrective aggregation (expert labeling of the states the learner itself reaches, developed below) when an expert can label a safe action there. If a pipeline trains on the entire recorded trajectory without curation, it commits learner-action contamination. The failing policy’s own commands enter the dataset as expert labels. The network then learns to answer the visual cues of an approaching hazard (such as the gripper sliding off the handle or the base drifting toward a rack upright) with the high-risk commands that produced them, which accelerates divergence rather than correcting it. Learner-action contamination corrupts the training targets directly by injecting errant commands, whereas causal confusion occurs when a policy trained on uncorrupted expert targets locks onto spurious correlations (Haan et al. 2019). The driving example from that work is a brake indicator on the dashboard. The light turns on because the expert braked, so it predicts the braking label almost perfectly, and a policy can learn to brake when the light is on rather than when an obstacle appears. At the latch, a measured joint current that climbs because the operator has already begun to push could play the same role, a consequence of the label that a policy mistakes for its cause.

Haan, Pim de, Dinesh Jayaraman, and Sergey Levine. 2019. “Causal Confusion in Imitation Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 32.

Separating these labels requires locating the divergence point where the autonomous policy departed the nominal path, rather than treating takeover as the first error. The divergence precedes the takeover by at least the time a supervisor needs to respond. A remote operator who was not watching continuously must notice the hazard, reorient to the scene, and take control. The running machine’s card in The running machine budgets that out-of-loop takeover at 4.47 s, from published take-over studies plus console video and handshake (Human reaction time and travel before braking derives it). Edge loggers therefore keep a circular ring buffer spanning a fixed lookback window \(\PreTriggerWindowDuration =\) 5 s, that takeover time rounded up to whole seconds. Because dynamic memory allocation during safety-critical events introduces non-deterministic latency spikes, the buffer is pre-allocated in locked memory (mlock) or non-volatile storage and populated deterministically at the permission tick rate (1 kHz). When a human safety supervisor hits an override button or an MCU safety enforcer trips a barrier, the pipeline latches the intervention timestamp \(\TakeoverInstant\) and freezes the preceding ring buffer. An out-of-loop supervisory takeover takes longer than the teleoperation loop’s continuous correction (section 5.6); at an illustrative 100 Hz, that delay spans hundreds of control cycles, all logged under the learner’s authority.

↪ The authority record, which logs on every control cycle which entity held authority and the root cause of any preemption, is developed in The Authority Record.

Detecting the divergence instant \(\DivergenceInstant\), estimated below as \(\EstimatedDivergenceInstant\), in offline ingestion pipelines requires searching backwards in time from the physical override timestamp across this synchronized pre-trigger telemetry buffer. For the latch, \(\TrajectoryDivergenceResidual(\TimeInstant)\) is the lateral gripper-position error between measured and planned kinematics in the declared fixture frame, and \(\DivergenceTolerance\) is its positive tolerance in meters. The divergence estimate is: \[\begin{aligned} \EstimatedDivergenceInstant = \min \Bigl\{ \TimeInstant \in [\TakeoverInstant - \PreTriggerWindowDuration, \TakeoverInstant] \;\Big|\; &|\TrajectoryDivergenceResidual(\TimeInstant)| > \DivergenceTolerance \\ &\lor\; \text{Tr}(\PolicyEnsembleCovariance(\TimeInstant)) > \EnsembleSpreadThreshold^2 \Bigr\}. \end{aligned} \tag{7}\] The pipeline scans the lookback window for the first threshold crossing. The policy ensemble covariance is computed after normalizing each action component by its declared scale, so its trace and variance threshold are dimensionless. In unstructured contact manipulation where no static reference trajectory exists, divergence detection instead evaluates declared physical residuals against admissible safety envelope boundaries, such as velocity-to-obstacle clearance margins or anomalous contact force derivatives \(d\ContactForceMagnitude/d\TimeInstant\). The detection thresholds are scenario-specific and calibrated to the sensor noise floor. The minimum defines an estimate only when the retained window contains a threshold crossing from below; an empty set or a residual already above threshold at the start of the window leaves the onset unresolved.

The protocol of intervention curation separates learner commands, expert labels on learner-visited states, and post-takeover recovery at timestamps the permission path logs for every takeover, the entries of the authority record that Supervisory Intervention formalizes. The takeover instant \(\TakeoverInstant\) is the permission tick at which the authority commit revokes the policy’s ownership of the arm, and the three curation rules below are measured from it:

  1. Quarantine drift commands: The actions issued by the failing autonomous policy during the drift window \([\DivergenceInstant, \TakeoverInstant)\) are retained in the forensic audit log with their safety flags, but masked out (\(\TrainingLabelMask = 0\)) from imitation training targets.
  2. Preserve drift states for relabeling: The physical states visited during \([\DivergenceInstant, \TakeoverInstant)\) capture off-nominal boundary conditions that are absent from nominal demonstrations. These states are admitted to training only when an automated expert, such as a planner with privileged state, supplies corrective labels (\(\DemonstrationActionLabel^*(\DecisionProcessState)\)); a person replaying a logged state cannot reliably say what they would have commanded while closing the loop on it.
  3. Admit only stabilized recovery: The post-takeover trajectory is not one demonstration. It opens with the enforcer’s blend, in which the enforcer, still the only writer to the drives, ramps its single admitted command smoothly from the learner’s last checked reference to the operator’s. The actuators therefore never see a step, and no two sources ever command them at once. The operator’s reorientation follows. Neither interval is expert behavior, and both stay masked. Only the segment after the operator’s measured stabilization \(\OperatorStabilizationInstant\), the instant the operator’s corrections settle into a steady return toward the corridor, up to recovery at \(\RecoveryCompletionInstant\), and only where the episode passes task-validity review, becomes an expert recovery demonstration. It teaches the policy how to steer from boundary states back to the nominal manifold. Causal masking and recovery eligibility defines the blend, reorientation, and stabilization intervals and the criterion that ends reorientation.

For the latch campaign, consider a stipulated follow-up collection, latch-recovery-02, at the positive 3 mm strike-plate offset and the guarded 0.03 m/s approach. The same 15 N tripwire remains active. An expert takes over from a misaligned approach, withdraws and reseats the gripper, then releases the latch. The stipulated archive contains valid, reviewed recovery sequences; this is a worked teaching branch, not a report of collected trials or measured improvement.

The three rules decide what this archive keeps. The unsuccessful learner commands are retained for diagnosis, expert relabeling is used only where a corrective target is available, and the expert’s recovery is admitted only from its stabilized, reviewed segment. A new provenance digest binds those labels to the displaced fixture, the original plant contract, and the collection safeguards. The original archive remains immutable. Its successor now contains experience for the positive-offset guarded cell, while the negative-offset and faster-approach cells remain known absences. Policy Training uses this archive to construct the candidate latch-bc-recovery-02, and closed-loop evaluation must test recovery and nominal-task regressions before any expanded competence claim follows.

↪ The qualification of latch-bc-recovery-02 against fresh nominal and displaced-plate trials is developed in Qualifying a recovery update.

The states between divergence and takeover are the second law, endogenous data, made concrete. The learner’s own control actions chose them, and the original nominal archive supplied no corrective supervision there; they are exactly the off-corridor states of figure 1, now visited and logged. Curated, they supply the missing return actions. Training on nominal expert demonstrations alone leaves the compounding-error problem of section 5.3 in place, bounded in the worst case by the quadratic growth of equation 1, \(\CovariateDriftBound\). Labeling the states the learner itself reaches changes how that bound grows.

In corrective aggregation, such as Dataset Aggregation (DAgger) (Ross et al. 2011), an expert labels states visited by the learner, including eligible pre-takeover drift states. Training on states sampled from the learner’s induced distribution (\(\DecisionProcessState \sim \LearnerStateVisitationDistribution\)), rather than solely the expert’s distribution (\(\DecisionProcessState \sim \ExpertStateVisitationDistribution\)), directly counters the covariate shift that nominal demonstrations leave unaddressed. The resulting performance guarantee depends on a constant determined by plant dynamics and task geometry rather than the learning algorithm. After \(\AggregationRoundCount\) rounds of aggregation, at least one policy \(\AggregationLearnedPolicy\) in the sequence satisfies \[\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\AggregationLearnedPolicy) \le \ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*) + \DeviationDisadvantage\,\BehaviorCloningHorizon\,\AggregatedSurrogateLoss + \mathcal{O}(1), \tag{8}\] where \(\AggregatedSurrogateLoss\) represents the surrogate loss of the best single policy on the aggregated learner-visited states (a different quantity from the per-step disagreement \(\SingleStepImitationError\) of equation 1). The symbol \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) is the recoverability constant, the maximum increase in the expert’s remaining cost-to-go incurred by a single errant action before the expert resumes control.10 This bound scales linearly with the task horizon \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\) only while \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) remains bounded, which is to say only while the expert can undo any single mistake at a bounded price. If \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) scales in proportion to \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\), the product \(\DeviationDisadvantage \BehaviorCloningHorizon\AggregatedSurrogateLoss\) reverts to \(\mathcal{O}(\BehaviorCloningHorizon^2\AggregatedSurrogateLoss)\), wiping out the linear advantage over behavioral cloning; Ross et al. (2011) observed this exact failure mode in their predecessor forward-training scheme.

10 Corrective Aggregation Error Bound: The bound is Theorem 3.2 of Ross et al. (2011). It holds for some policy in the sequence of \(\AggregationRoundCount\) iterations, with \(\AggregationRoundCount\) of order \(\DeviationDisadvantage \BehaviorCloningHorizon\) up to logarithmic factors, and \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) bounds the change in the expert’s cost-to-go after any single action. Imitation Learning and Compounding Covariate Shift states the finite-sample form, in which the same constant appears as \(\Glyph{u_{\max}}\).

Ross, Stéphane, Geoffrey Gordon, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2011. “A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 15: 627–35.

For a horizon of \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon =\) 500 steps (5 s at 100 Hz) and \(\SingleStepImitationError =\) 0.001, the behavioral cloning bound \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon^2\SingleStepImitationError =\) 250. The aggregation term \(\DeviationDisadvantage\,\BehaviorCloningHorizon\AggregatedSurrogateLoss\) in equation 8 evaluates to 0.5 for a recoverable task with \(\DeviationDisadvantage = 1\) and matching per-step error. In this setting, the quadratic cloning bound equals half the episode’s maximum budget, while the leading aggregation term is less than the cost of a single control step. This comparison is conceptual, since the two error terms evaluate losses over different state distributions and both bounds omit problem-dependent leading constants. The quadratic bound becomes vacuous whenever \(\SingleStepImitationError\) exceeds \(1/\BehaviorCloningHorizon =\) 0.002, at which point \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon^2\SingleStepImitationError\) exceeds the maximum possible episode cost of \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\). Figure 7 joins the two halves of this section, the feedback loop that makes cloning’s worst case quadratic and the curation timeline that turns one takeover into labels the linear bound can use.

The worst case, where \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) grows in proportion to the remaining horizon, is the first law, irreversibility (principle 1), written in the language of imitation bounds. Once the plant enters an unrecoverable state, such as a bent latch, a sheared cable harness, or a dropped brittle workpiece, no subsequent expert command can restore the lost objective. In these failure modes, the cost penalty spans the entire remaining task horizon (\(\DeviationDisadvantage \sim \BehaviorCloningHorizon\)), and aggregating additional learner states cannot recover linear error bounds. A policy cannot learn recovery from states where recovery is physically impossible; the deterministic permission path must prevent entry into those states altogether. The latch campaign draws this boundary explicitly. Withdrawing and reseating a misaligned gripper is recoverable, so latch-recovery-02 records corrections there. Contact forces approaching the illustrative 100 N component damage threshold are unrecoverable; the illustrative 15 N tripwire is a detection threshold, not a contact-force ceiling. Its use requires a qualified collection-response envelope accounting for detection, command and actuator response, and peak-force overshoot below the damage threshold. Without that evidence, the lower threshold alone cannot authorize collection. Those damaging states belong to the safety-enforcer domain rather than the policy learning targets.

Gathering expert supervision on learner-visited states takes several architectural forms, and the curation rules detailed above instantiate one of them. Classical DAgger requires an expert to query and label every state visited during autonomous execution, an impossible requirement for a human supervisor when the policy issues commands at control rates. The framework of human-gated aggregation resolves this bottleneck by allowing a human supervisor to take over only when necessary, appending labels exclusively from the resulting intervention episodes (Kelly et al. 2019). The three curation rules refine this approach by keeping quarantined learner commands, relabeled drift states, and expert recovery actions under separate provenance. Where standard human-gated aggregation retains the entire human takeover stream, rule 3 also filters out the transitional controller blend and operator reorientation intervals. Two refinements address the scarcity of supervisor attention. One queries the expert only when policy uncertainty or novelty exceeds a threshold, spending a fixed intervention budget where the policy is least sure (Hoque et al. 2022). The other upweights the sparse recovery segments relative to the dense nominal trajectories during training, so a few minutes of rescue are not drowned by hours of nominal motion (Mandlekar et al. 2020).

Hoque, Ryan, Ashwin Balakrishna, Ellen Novoseller, Albert Wilcox, Daniel S. Brown, and Ken Goldberg. 2022. “ThriftyDAgger: Budget-Aware Novelty and Risk Gating for Interactive Imitation Learning.” Proceedings of the Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 164: 598–608.
Mandlekar, Ajay, Danfei Xu, Roberto Martín-Martín, Yuke Zhu, Li Fei-Fei, and Silvio Savarese. 2020. “Human-in-the-Loop Imitation Learning Using Remote Teleoperation.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2012.06733.

A team can also avoid online interventions entirely, and this is the wide-tube remedy promised in section 5.3. Injecting small noise perturbations into the teleoperator’s commands pushes the robot off the nominal path during ordinary collection, so the operator’s corrections back toward the corridor appear in the initial demonstration set itself (Laskey et al. 2017). Each perturbation consumes hardware life from the fatigue budget in section 5.6.3 and must stay within recoverable bounds. Ingestion pipelines therefore log the collection mode of every episode explicitly (nominal, noise-injected, or gated intervention), because the three modes put different kinds of states and labels into the archive.

Learner-action contamination compounds across policy generations. Suppose a first policy drifts toward a joint torque limit until a supervisor intervenes with an override pendant. A successor trained on those uncurated drift-window commands as expert demonstrations reinforces the behavior that produced the departure, drives harder toward the limit in deployment, and forces operators to intervene earlier and more often. A pipeline that monitors only offline validation loss misses the cascade, because loss on the mislabeled validation split keeps improving as the network memorizes the corrupt labels while physical stability degrades.

Preventing this cascade requires the permission path to record every takeover or safety-barrier trip as an explicit entry in the authority record.11 A boolean disengagement tag overlaid on video is not enough. The ingestion pipeline requires a populated pre-trigger ring buffer matching the declared lookback window (5 s), hardware-synchronized sensor observations and actuator taps, monotonic override timestamps, unique authority identifiers, and precise timestamps marking the blend completion and supervisor stabilization. Without this synchronized pre-trigger telemetry, ingestion pipelines cannot locate the divergence point \(\DivergenceInstant\) and therefore cannot isolate errant learner commands from the boundary states eligible for expert relabeling.

11 Event Data Recorder: US vehicle rules under 49 CFR Part 563 require an automotive event data recorder to preserve a fixed window of telemetry recorded prior to a crash trigger, recognizing that the root cause of an incident precedes the trigger event itself (National Highway Traffic Safety Administration 2024). Human supervisory takeovers have the same temporal structure, because the initial divergence \(\DivergenceInstant\) always precedes the override timestamp \(\TakeoverInstant\). A logging pipeline that begins capturing telemetry only upon trigger activation has already discarded the critical drift data required for policy curation.

National Highway Traffic Safety Administration. 2024. Event Data Recorders: Final Rule (49 CFR Part 563). 89 FR 102810. U.S. Department of Transportation.
Checkpoint 5.3: Intervention curation and error bounds

Before curating human intervention logs for policy retraining, audit compounding error dynamics and timestamp attribution against these criteria:

Even with expert labels on learner-visited states, the resulting demonstration archive remains tethered to the kinematics and dynamics of the collecting machine. Pooling demonstrations across robot arms, bases, and end-effectors raises the cross-embodiment questions in section 5.9.

5.9 Cross-Embodiment Transfer

Borrowing manipulation demonstrations from another robot arm looks effortless in tensor space, because both datasets store image frames and seven-element action vectors. That ease disappears when a trajectory recorded on a wide-stroke, \(50\text{ Hz}\) gripper drives a narrower, \(20\text{ Hz}\) gripper into its mechanical endstops or misses a moving conveyor part by centimeters. Physical demonstration trajectories never represent a task in the abstract. Every recorded stream of sensor observations and actuator commands embeds the link lengths, joint velocity limits, camera optical paths, bus latencies, and contact-surface compliances of the logging hardware directly into its numbers. Reusing demonstration datasets across robots therefore requires separating environmental task invariants from machine-specific realizations, checking kinematic and dynamic compatibility, and resolving topological mismatches. This applies whenever the warehouse manipulator supplements its local latch demonstrations with data from foreign arms and grippers.

5.9.1 Embodiment contracts and the normalization fallacy

Suppose a training pipeline rescales gripper commands from two different robots to the unit interval \([0, 1]\) so a single network head can predict both. A normalized command that opens a wide-stroke donor gripper far enough to clear a workpiece will open a narrower target gripper to only a fraction of that span, driving its fingers straight into the part. That failure occurs because an observation-action trajectory \((\MultimodalObservation_{t}, \DemonstrationActionLabel_t)\) permanently encodes the embodiment contract between a specific machine and its physical surroundings. This contract covers the sensor geometry and optical calibration and the sampling period and transport latency of the perception bus. It also covers the coordinate frames and kinematic limits of the actuators, the torque-speed saturation curves of the motors (including reflected rotor inertia \(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\) in geared transmissions), and the friction and compliance of the contact interface. For the running machine’s recorded teleoperation, the contract specifies a rolling-shutter navigation camera at 4056 by 3040 px and 60 Hz, a global-shutter wrist camera at 1440 by 1080 px on its dedicated bracket. The contract also specifies a 9-axis kinematic log sampled at the 1 kHz permission tick (notebook 5.2). Every latch demonstration in that archive reflects that camera suite, that sensor bracket, and that exact arm. Altering any parameter in this contract alters the mapping between commanded inputs and physical state transitions. Even when two robots follow an identical Cartesian tool trajectory \(\ToolCartesianPose(\TimeInstant)\), their required actuator torques diverge. Written per joint, Newton’s second law for a serial manipulator yields the manipulator equation, met earlier as the model classical control inverts: \[\JointTorque = \GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\GeneralizedAccelerations + \CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\GeneralizedVelocities + \GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates) + \ExternalContactTorque,\] where \(\GeneralizedCoordinates\) denotes joint positions, \(\GeneralizedInertiaMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) is the configuration-dependent mass matrix, \(\CoriolisCentrifugalMatrix(\GeneralizedCoordinates, \GeneralizedVelocities)\) collects Coriolis and centrifugal forces, \(\GravitationalTorqueVector(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) is the gravitational torque vector, and \(\ExternalContactTorque\) represents torques exerted by external contact forces. Rigid-body dynamics and continuous collision checks builds this equation up from the single-joint case. Because every term depends directly on link masses, rotor inertias, and gear ratios, the same tool trajectory demands different torque profiles across different robot embodiments.

↩︎ The manipulator equation, which classical control inverts to compute joint torques, is established in The Classical Wall.

↩︎ Reflected rotor inertia, \(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\), and its effect on joint dynamics are established in Reflected rotor inertia and optimal inertia matching.

While the embodiment contract defines the physics of a single machine, the cross-embodiment compatibility contract determines whether trajectories logged under one embodiment contract can safely serve as action targets on another. This compatibility contract defines the forward kinematics mapping (translating joint angles to tool pose), control-rate transformations (\(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\)), and actuator effort bounds. These bounds prevent a high-force profile or rapid spatial velocity demonstrated on a rigid industrial arm from driving a lightweight cobot beyond its structural limits. Large pooled corpora rarely provide such guarantees. Open X-Embodiment maps action fields into a coarsely aligned seven-dimensional end-effector format and normalizes each dataset independently. However, it deliberately leaves coordinate frames unaligned and allows action vectors to represent either absolute or relative poses and velocities across different sub-corpora (Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al. 2024). A multi-task model trained on this pool learns to interpret identical action vectors differently based on embodiment tokens. In contrast, DROID sidesteps embodiment mismatch altogether by collecting all data on a single standardized robot model (Khazatsky et al. 2024). Demonstrations from diverse robots can enrich representation learning without an explicit compatibility contract, but such a contract becomes strictly mandatory the moment borrowed actions are imitated directly as supervisory labels or dispatched to a physical plant.

How much work the compatibility contract has to do is visible in the largest pooled robot corpus. The Open X-Embodiment collection merges independently recorded datasets into one training mixture and publishes, for each, its episode count, robot morphology, scene type, and command rate (Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al. 2024). Figure 8 weights each dataset by its episodes.

Of the 2.4 million episodes across 72 datasets, 91 percent come from single arms and 89 percent from table-top scenes. Mobile manipulators, the class of the running machine, contribute 5 percent, and two datasets supply 95 percent of those. The command rates are as uneven: 57 percent of episodes were logged at 10 Hz, 28 percent declare no rate, and only 2 percent were recorded at or above the machine’s 20 Hz chunk rate. Corpus size therefore says little about coverage of one machine’s contract. Almost every episode in the mixture needs the rate transformation and morphology mapping of the compatibility contract before it can serve as an action target here.

A data-reuse pipeline must separate environmental task invariants from embodiment-specific realizations. A task invariant is a geometric or dynamical condition imposed by the physical process, for example bringing a compliant probe into contact with a terminal pad within \(5^\circ\) of the surface normal while sustaining a normal contact force between \(2.0\text{ N}\) and \(5.0\text{ N}\). An embodiment realization is the concrete sequence of joint encoder counts, pulse-width modulation duty cycles, perception transport latencies, and sensor pixel rasters used to achieve that condition on a particular test rig. While the contact goal exists in world coordinates, the dataset stores only the realization. When a model trains directly on raw hardware realizations, it internalizes the idiosyncratic mechanics of the source testbed, including structural flexure, drive backlash, and sensor mounting oscillations.

Consider transferring a demonstration dataset collected on Machine A to Machine B for an identical pick-and-place task:

  • Machine A operates a parallel-jaw gripper with a stroke of \(0\text{ to }100\text{ mm}\), a \(50\text{ Hz}\) command rate (\(20\text{ ms}\) period), a continuous grasp force limit of \(40\text{ N}\), and a wrist camera mounted \(45\text{ mm}\) behind the tool center point with a \(15\text{ ms}\) transport delay.
  • Machine B operates a compact gripper with a stroke of \(0\text{ to }60\text{ mm}\), a \(20\text{ Hz}\) command rate (\(50\text{ ms}\) period), a continuous force limit of \(15\text{ N}\), and a wrist camera mounted \(20\text{ mm}\) behind the tool center point with a \(35\text{ ms}\) transport delay.

Projecting Machine A’s demonstrations directly through the physical constraints of Machine B reveals immediate points of hardware failure. For a workpiece of width \(75\text{ mm}\), Machine A commands a gripper aperture of \(80\text{ mm}\). That command exceeds Machine B’s mechanical stroke by \(20\text{ mm}\), driving its leadscrew into the mechanical endstop. During the hold phase, Machine A applies \(30\text{ N}\) of clamping force; executing that command on Machine B doubles its continuous rating of \(15\text{ N}\) and forces its motor amplifier into current saturation. In the time domain, subsampling the \(50\text{ Hz}\) trajectory onto Machine B’s \(20\text{ Hz}\) bus aliases rapid contact dynamics and increases the half-period average hold age \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant / 2\) from \(10\text{ ms}\) to \(25\text{ ms}\), introducing \(15\text{ ms}\) of additional zero-order hold phase lag (Discrete-time sampling and zero-order hold phase lag). Compounded by the \(20\text{ ms}\) difference in camera transport delay (\(35\text{ ms} - 15\text{ ms}\)), the added low-frequency phase-equivalent delay is \(35\text{ ms}\), of which the camera difference contributes \(20\text{ ms}\). At the assumed constant approach velocity of \(0.10\text{ m/s}\), that phase approximation corresponds to an equivalent displacement of \(3.5\text{ mm}\). Actual contact overrun requires measured or bounded sampling phase, capture and transport, computation, actuation, and plant response; the phase-equivalent value does not establish when the first correction dispatches. Meanwhile, the \(25\text{ mm}\) disparity in wrist camera mounting introduces optical parallax that shifts the apparent position of the workpiece boundary across the camera raster.

Standard machine learning pipelines often try to reconcile these discrepancies by normalizing observation and action channels to \([0, 1]\) or \([-1, 1]\). That rescaling aligns tensor ranges while leaving the physical mismatch intact. Normalizing gripper travel to \([0, 1]\) turns Machine A’s safe \(80\text{ mm}\) opening (\(\NormalizedActionCommand = 0.80\)) into a \(48\text{ mm}\) opening on Machine B (\(0.80 \times 60\text{ mm}\)) that drives its fingers into the \(75\text{ mm}\) workpiece during approach. Normalizing feature ranges rescales numbers, but it cannot lengthen a leadscrew, lower motor coil resistance, or recover bus transport delay. Demonstration trajectories plotted in normalized unit spaces may look identical, yet only the subset inside the physical intersection (apertures below \(60\text{ mm}\), forces under \(15\text{ N}\), and command frequencies below \(20\text{ Hz}\)) can run on the target hardware without saturation or contact damage. Even within that intersection, two arms with different joint layouts can collide with the workspace when they share a single Cartesian command (section 5.9.2).

5.9.2 Kinematic topology mismatches and null-space projection

Even when end-effectors, force ratings, and control rates align, differences in arm geometry can break a transferred trajectory. When a 7-DoF arm’s demonstration is compressed into a 6-DoF Cartesian hand displacement so it can be pooled with other robots, the log discards the arm’s elbow posture; replaying that 6-DoF stream through a target robot’s inverse kinematics solver can swing the elbow into a shelf upright or drive a lighter arm through a singularity, even though the hand follows the exact demonstrated path. Combining robots with different kinematic structures introduces two topological conflicts:

  1. Action space incompatibility: A 7-DoF arm records joint positions \(\GeneralizedCoordinates \in \mathbb{R}^7\), a bimanual workstation records \(\GeneralizedCoordinates \in \mathbb{R}^{14}\), and a mobile base logs planar velocities \((\BaseLongitudinalVelocity, \BaseLateralVelocity, \BaseHeadingRate)\). Because joint-space commands cannot transfer directly across distinct kinematic arrangements, multi-embodiment models project action sequences into a common Cartesian end-effector displacement: \[\FiniteChange \ToolCartesianPose_t = (\FiniteChange \EndEffectorFirstAxisPosition, \FiniteChange \EndEffectorSecondAxisPosition, \FiniteChange \EndEffectorThirdAxisPosition, \FiniteChange \EndEffectorRollAngle, \FiniteChange \EndEffectorPitchAngle, \FiniteChange \EndEffectorYawAngle) \in \mathbb{R}^{6}, \quad \GripperOpeningCommand \in [0, 1],\] where the final three elements represent local roll, pitch, and yaw increments under a declared rotation convention. This displacement vector is not itself a member of the Special Euclidean group \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\); any numerical integration must handle angle wrapping and representation singularities. Projecting into Cartesian space standardizes spatial geometry, but it strips away actuator dynamics. A Cartesian step that an industrial arm executes effortlessly near its nominal pose can force a lighter arm through a kinematic singularity, a configuration in which aligned joints leave the tool unable to move in some direction. The manipulator Jacobian \(\ManipulatorJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\), a configuration-dependent matrix that Mapping tool motion to joint limits develops on the machine’s own arm, maps joint velocities to tool spatial velocities through \(\ToolCartesianVelocity = \ManipulatorJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\GeneralizedVelocities\). When \(\ManipulatorJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) loses row rank at a singularity, no finite joint velocity can generate motion along the lost Cartesian direction. Commanded displacements near such poses demand joint velocities that immediately exceed the target machine’s actuator velocity bounds.
  2. Null-space projection and elbow swivel ambiguity: Away from singularities, mapping a 7-DoF arm into a 6-DoF Cartesian delta projects out the one-dimensional null space (\(7 - 6 = 1\) redundant degree of freedom). For a redundant manipulator with Jacobian \(\ManipulatorJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \in \mathbb{R}^{6 \times 7}\), the general kinematic relationship between Cartesian velocity \(\ToolCartesianVelocity\) and joint velocity \(\GeneralizedVelocities\) is: \[\GeneralizedVelocities = \JacobianPseudoinverse(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ToolCartesianVelocity + \left( \IdentityMatrix - \JacobianPseudoinverse(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \ManipulatorJacobian(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \right) \NullSpaceVelocityBias,\] where \(\JacobianPseudoinverse = \ManipulatorJacobian^\top (\ManipulatorJacobian \ManipulatorJacobian^\top)^{-1}\) is the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse (the minimum-norm joint velocity producing \(\ToolCartesianVelocity\) whenever \(\ManipulatorJacobian\) has full row rank), and the projection operator \((\IdentityMatrix - \JacobianPseudoinverse \ManipulatorJacobian)\) filters an arbitrary joint-velocity bias \(\NullSpaceVelocityBias \in \mathbb{R}^7\) into self-motion that leaves the end-effector still. For an anthropomorphic arm with shoulder and wrist geometry that admits this construction, a fixed end-effector pose in \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) and a fixed shoulder anchor leave an elbow orbit around the shoulder-to-wrist axis, parameterized by the elbow swivel angle \(\ElbowSwivelAngle\). A general redundant arm instead requires its own configuration parameter or the full joint vector; one redundant degree of freedom does not imply this circular geometry. Discarding this internal degree of freedom to impose a uniform 6-DoF action representation erases the human operator’s postural intent. When the target robot receives this 6-DoF command stream, its local inverse kinematics (IK) solver must resolve the redundancy using an arbitrary mathematical heuristic, such as joint-limit centering or manipulability maximization. Without the original swivel angle, the solver may adopt an elbow posture that drives joints into hard stops or sweeps the elbow into nearby shelving, fixtures, or personnel during a motion that was entirely collision-free in the source environment. Preserving the applicable redundancy parameter, or the complete joint-state vector, within the trajectory’s provenance metadata retains the operator’s postural intent for target-specific retargeting and collision checks. The running mobile manipulator archive maintains this intent directly: its arm has redundant kinematics, and its logger captures all 9 axes of the arm and base at the 1 kHz permission tick. Any pooled 6-DoF delta generated from a latch-servicing trajectory can always be traced back to the demonstrator’s original elbow posture.

↪ Forward kinematics, the manipulator Jacobian, its singularities, and the pseudoinverse are developed in Forward kinematics and singularities.

Reusing physical data across different robots therefore requires two validation gates at the ingestion boundary, corresponding to two distinct roles in training:

  • The pretraining admission gate evaluates whether a trajectory can safely contribute to general representation learning. This gate validates data provenance, observation integrity, coordinate frame conventions, action semantics (reference frame, absolute vs. relative representations, position vs. velocity modes), and sampling rates. In particular, source actions admitted at this gate need not be physically executable on the target robot. Multi-embodiment foundation models rely on this gate to ingest broad visual and task structures across dozens of robot morphologies before fine-tuning on target hardware (Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al. 2024; Black et al. 2024).
  • The target-label gate applies whenever recorded actions serve as direct imitation labels or execution setpoints on the physical plant. This gate maps the source trajectory through target forward kinematics to verify coordinate consistency (Lynch and Park 2017). It confirms that commanded joint velocities and accelerations remain within the target plant’s dynamic envelope (Tedrake 2023). It also verifies that task-critical features remain within the field of view and dynamic range of the target sensors. When commanded trajectories violate kinematic reach or motor torque limits, an ingestion pipeline cannot simply clamp the values to target boundaries. Clamping distorts velocity profiles, alters phase relationships, and destroys the causal link between observed spatial errors and corrective control outputs. Trajectories that satisfy the target dynamics pass untouched; trajectories that admit valid geometric retargeting through inverse kinematics are relabeled; and trajectories that demand unobtainable actuator authority or rely on unobservable states are rejected.
Lynch, Kevin M, and Frank C Park. 2017. Modern Robotics: Mechanics, Planning, and Control. Cambridge University Press.
Tedrake, Russ. 2023. Robotic Manipulation: Perception, Planning, and Control. Massachusetts Institute of Technology.

Evaluating the two-gripper scenario of section 5.9.1 through this target-label gate resolves each trajectory segment systematically. The initial opening phase must be rejected rather than relabeled, because satisfying the task invariant (clearing the \(75\text{ mm}\) workpiece) demands an aperture that Machine B cannot physically achieve. The \(30\text{ N}\) grasp hold is rejected on identical grounds, as it exceeds Machine B’s continuous motor rating. Conversely, the \(50\text{ Hz}\) trajectory can be resampled and relabeled onto Machine B’s \(20\text{ Hz}\) control bus only if the slower command stream preserves the task-critical contact dynamics and remains within the target’s verified tracking and force limits. The data record must also log the added zero-order hold lag and camera transport delay so that policy training accounts for the altered timing. An ingestion pipeline lacking this gate injects dynamically invalid trajectories into the training distribution. A policy trained on such unvetted data fails in deployment not because of neural network underfitting, but because its training labels were physically valid only on the donor hardware. For the latch-servicing campaign, the target plant is the mobile manipulator’s own arm and gripper. Every borrowed trajectory proposed as a latch training label must pass this gate against the mobile manipulator’s verified limit record, regardless of the donor platform’s reported capabilities.

↩︎ The limit record, which states each measured limit with its operating conditions, uncertainty, and margin, is established in The six elements of a limit record.

The warehouse conveyor pick demonstrates a transfer mismatch rooted in task dynamics rather than robot kinematics. At the sorting station, the arm grasps a workpiece moving along a belt at 0.20 m/s. The workpiece translates 5 mm during each 25 ms control step and advances 80 mm over a single 16-step action chunk of 400 ms, a displacement of 5.3× the allowable 15 mm grasp tolerance. A borrowed tabletop demonstration recorded on a stationary bench defines its grasp trajectory entirely in the static table frame. If a policy imitates that trajectory unchanged at the conveyor, an open-loop action chunk aims at the spatial location the part occupied when the chunk was generated. The target-label gate can adapt such a static trajectory only by transforming the end-effector path into the moving workpiece coordinate frame. This coordinate transformation requires both the instantaneous object pose in the donor metadata and the conveyor velocity in the target environment record. An external trajectory lacking this telemetry can enter the pipeline solely through the pretraining admission gate. Teleoperation data collected directly on the warehouse machine requires no such mathematical rescue, as the human operator generated the demonstration commands with the conveyor in motion.

5.9.3 Choosing pooled experience for the target task

Pretraining on a million pooled trajectories from foreign robots does not help the warehouse manipulator open a displaced latch if the training mixture drowns out the machine’s own contact dynamics. The same holds if the mixture adds ten thousand repetitions of a single tabletop scene instead of covering new operating cells. Large-scale pooled corpora such as DROID (Khazatsky et al. 2024), BridgeData v2 (Walke et al. 2023), and Open X-Embodiment (Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al. 2024) offer broad scene diversity and multi-robot demonstrations, but their value to a specific deployment depends on what information survives the admission gates of section 5.9.2. Even when kinematic mismatches prevent direct imitation of donor joint commands, foreign demonstrations can supply visual representations, object affordances, and high-level task structures, provided the campaign measures how those additions fill its scenario table.

Belkhale, Suneel, Yuchen Cui, and Dorsa Sadigh. 2023. “Data Quality in Imitation Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 80375–95. https://doi.org/10.52202/075280-3524.

Empirical scaling studies since 2024 support the premise behind the scenario table, that policies generalize with the number of distinct environmental conditions in their data rather than with repetitions of the same ones. In an extensive empirical evaluation spanning more than 40,000 demonstrations collected with handheld grippers, policy success across novel environments and unseen objects scaled as a power law with the count of distinct training environments and objects. Adding further demonstrations within existing environments yielded sharply diminishing returns once a modest baseline was achieved. A dataset collecting 50 demonstrations across each of 32 environments achieved 90 percent on novel environment-object pairs (Lin et al. 2025). Similarly, evaluating mobile manipulation across residential settings revealed steady performance gains as the training distribution expanded from 3 to 104 homes. At 104 locations, the generalized model matched the performance of an in-domain baseline trained exclusively within the evaluation homes (Black et al. 2025). Viewed through the scenario table, the primary scaling axis is the count of distinct operational cells populated by the corpus. The declared minimum coverage threshold \(\MinimumCellSupportCount\) identifies cells that still need collection; it does not establish that additional trajectories within a populated cell have no value. Populating operational cells is necessary but not sufficient on its own. As Belkhale et al. (2023) demonstrate, dataset utility depends on two additional quality metrics: action divergence (the discrepancy between expert actions and learned policy outputs at reachable states) and transition diversity (the stochasticity of outcomes from given state-action pairs). Uncurated state diversity does not automatically yield performance improvements.

These demonstration corpora also diverge in how they acquire operational diversity. Open X-Embodiment aggregates 60 distinct datasets across 22 different robotic embodiments with different, unaligned action spaces; despite this lack of structural alignment, joint pretraining improved success rates across the smaller constituent datasets (Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al. 2024). DROID and AgiBot World instead hold the embodiment contract constant while maximizing environmental and task diversity. DROID deploys a single manipulator model across 564 in-the-wild environments (Khazatsky et al. 2024). AgiBot World scales this principle with a homogeneous fleet of more than 100 identical robots, producing pretraining data that demonstrated superior downstream transfer compared to Open X-Embodiment (AgiBot-World-Contributors et al. 2025). A homogeneous fleet removes nominal kinematic mismatches, but each unit must still satisfy the target-label gate through verified calibration and dynamic compatibility. The fleet also remains vulnerable to the shared kinematic blind spots of that specific platform. Conversely, a multi-robot corpus can broaden perceptual learning through the pretraining gate; its actions become target labels only after passing the target-label gate. Balancing these disparate data streams requires deliberate weighting within the training mixture; dynamically weighting datasets to safeguard the worst-performing target domain significantly outperforms uniform data sampling across Open X-Embodiment (Hejna et al. 2025).

Hejna, Joey, Chethan Anand Bhateja, Yichen Jiang, Karl Pertsch, and Dorsa Sadigh. 2025. “ReMix: Optimizing Data Mixtures for Large Scale Imitation Learning.” Proceedings of the 8th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 270: 145–64.
Levine, Sergey, Peter Pastor, Alex Krizhevsky, Julian Ibarz, and Deirdre Quillen. 2018. “Learning Hand-Eye Coordination for Robotic Grasping with Deep Learning and Large-Scale Data Collection.” The International Journal of Robotics Research 37 (4-5): 421–36.
Kalashnikov, Dmitry, Alex Irpan, Peter Pastor, Julian Ibarz, Alexander Herzog, Eric Jang, Deirdre Quillen, et al. 2018. “Scalable Deep Reinforcement Learning for Vision-Based Robotic Manipulation.” Proceedings of the 2nd Conference on Robot Learning, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 87: 651–73.
Black, Kevin, Noah Brown, Danny Driess, Adnan Esmail, Michael Equi, Chelsea Finn, Niccolo Fusai, et al. 2024. “\(\pi_0\): A Vision-Language-Action Flow Model for General Robot Control.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2410.24164.

Placing these landmark robot archives alongside community uploads and web-scale foundation-model corpora exposes both how physical collection has scaled and where its ceiling sits (figure 9). Across 11 major physical collection campaigns since 2015 (figure 9, panel a), robot datasets progressed from single-lab grasping farms (50,000 to 800,000 trials in a fixed bin (Levine et al. 2018; Kalashnikov et al. 2018)) through multi-scene teleoperation (60,096 trajectories in BridgeData V2 and 76,000 in DROID (Walke et al. 2023; Khazatsky et al. 2024)) to million-episode pooled and fleet archives (2,419,193 episodes in Open X-Embodiment and 1,001,552 in AgiBot World (Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al. 2024; AgiBot-World-Contributors et al. 2025)). Those pooled campaigns sit five orders of magnitude above the 8–15 episode median of single-session community uploads in figure 5. Even million-episode robot campaigns, however, confront the cross-domain gap in panel b. Across 902 language, vision, and robotics models with disclosed training dataset sizes in the Epoch AI catalog (Epoch AI 2026), frontier language and vision corpora reach \(10^{13}\) to \(10^{14}\) tokens or images, whereas \(\pi_0\)’s \(10{,}000\)-hour robot pretraining corpus (1,000,000,000 state-action steps (Black et al. 2024)) sits 14,800× below DeepSeek-V3’s 14.8 trillion tokens.

Because every physical trajectory is throttled by real-time motor execution and session yield (equation 6), robot learning cannot close a four-order-of-magnitude data gap by brute-force teleoperation alone. Deployed pipelines instead inherit broad semantic priors from web-pretrained vision-language backbones, use synthetic expansion or co-training where provenance allows, and spend their scarce physical episodes on the cells that scenario-table coverage and compatibility gates confirm they need, as the benchmark systems in table 5 illustrate.

The core engineering decision is whether incorporating compatible pooled experience improves target-task policy performance relative to an identical baseline trained strictly on in-domain data. Rigorous evaluation requires fixing the target task distribution and test protocol, maintaining full data provenance, and benchmarking both nominal task completion and recovery behavior. Merely increasing the number of embodiments in a dataset does not guarantee positive transfer. In the mobile manipulator’s displaced-latch campaign, the control baseline is a policy trained exclusively on 5,000 retained teleoperation demonstrations from the machine itself, populating a single cell within the six-cell scenario table. An expanded multi-robot corpus justifies its training overhead and ingestion complexity only if the resulting policy improves robustness on long-tail target conditions without regressing nominal latch-servicing performance.

While cross-embodiment compatibility contracts guard the ingestion boundary against hardware mismatches, demonstration data collected on the native machine can fail through internal mechanisms. Teleoperator drift, sensor calibration drift, fixture flexure, and misclassified execution failures corrupt physical datasets without altering tensor dimensions or triggering schema errors (section 5.10).

5.10 Dataset Degradation and Physical Distribution Shift

A demonstration archive can pass every schema check and fill its scenario table on paper while silently rotting from within. A technician may bump a camera mount by \(3\text{ mm}\) between shifts, a hot motor winding may lose peak torque during an afternoon session, or a curation script may mistake dropped force-sensor packets for a clean trajectory. Because physical datasets capture closed-loop interactions between controllers and hardware rather than static observations, verifying an archive requires auditing the mechanical, optical, and temporal meaning of recorded trajectories alongside array shapes and scenario-table counts.

5.10.1 Silent plant drift and kinematic reprojection verification

Suppose afternoon latch demonstrations begin missing the strike plate by two millimeters even though the same operator is running the same cell. Before an engineer can decide whether to relabel the afternoon runs, recalibrate the wrist camera, or service the arm, the pipeline must separate three sources of drift that occur inside a single supported scenario cell. These are changes in the physical plant, changes in the human operator’s control habits, and silent shifts in sensor calibration.

Passive covariate shift vs. physical dynamics shift

In those afternoon runs, the first question is whether the world around the arm changed or the arm itself changed, which is the distinction between passive observation shift and active plant drift introduced in section 5.2. Shifting afternoon sunlight across the cage door alters camera pixel intensities without changing how the joints respond to a torque command, whereas hot motor windings, worn gearbox teeth, abraded gripper pads, and varying door friction alter the plant dynamics directly. Before an ingestion pipeline pools or partitions demonstration episodes, it must determine which underlying physical conditions have shifted. Motor heating shows why the answer depends on where in its envelope the machine operated. A hot winding has higher resistance, which leaves the drive less voltage headroom. Inside the current-regulated region of the torque-speed envelope the drive still delivers the commanded torque, so the modeled voltage-headroom effect alone does not distinguish slow, hot and slow, cold demonstrations; other thermal and plant changes still require checking. Near the voltage limit at high joint speed, the same resistance rise caps the current and lowers the available torque, as section 5.2 described, so a fast demonstration recorded hot reflects a weaker plant than one recorded cold.

↩︎ The torque-speed envelope and the back-EMF roll-off at high speed are established in Torque-speed operating envelopes and back-EMF roll-off.

That dependence is why pipelines stratify episodes before pooling them. Stratifying by measured actuator temperatures, maintenance logs, and workpiece wear enables engineers to verify whether identical actuation commands yield consistent plant dynamics. The standard diagnostic tool for this verification is a repeatable excitation trajectory. At the start of each operating shift, the manipulator replays a predetermined joint-space trajectory while stationary. If the tracking error residual exceeds an established threshold, the pipeline flags the plant as mechanically altered. Logging timestamps alone cannot establish that a machine degraded during an operational shift; dataset curation pipelines must justify pooling across sessions based on verified physical invariants and intended training objectives rather than superficial uniformity of array dimensions.

Demonstration collection policies drift just as readily as mechanical hardware. Across 141.4 h of scheduled cell time, teleoperators naturally adapt their control strategies. An operator who initially moved cautiously, executing small corrective sub-movements and pausing deliberately before striking a latch plate, gradually develops muscle memory. Over time, that operator rounds corners, anticipates contact forces, and exploits system momentum. These mature demonstrations rely on feedforward compensation and tight timing rather than reflecting changes in joint friction. Partitioning datasets into temporal windows and monitoring episode durations, corrective jerk counts, command amplitudes, and teleoperator intervention rates exposes these subtle shifts in human behavior.
