This behavioral tracking reveals whether later trajectories contribute advanced manipulation skills or introduce an entirely distinct behavioral regime that requires segregated dataset splits or specialized behavioral labels.

Sensor calibration also deteriorates silently across operating sessions. Standard storage containers such as HDF5, Zarr, and Apache Parquet preserve array topologies, data types, and chunk structures, but they cannot verify whether stored coordinates correspond to physical reality. If a robot is serviced, bumped by personnel, or re-zeroed between collection sessions, joint encoder offsets and external camera poses shift. A camera mount displaced by merely \(3\text{ mm}\) moves workpiece pixels across image frames while the stored arrays remain syntactically valid. Clock synchronization drift across distributed fieldbus controllers introduces comparable corruption, skewing the temporal alignment between visual frames and tactile force measurements by tens of milliseconds.

↩︎ Distributed-clock synchronization across fieldbus drive nodes is established in Event-triggered and time-triggered bus architectures.

12 Camera Calibration: In practice \(\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix\) is estimated from a few views of a planar checkerboard, the method Zhang (2000) introduced, and the extrinsics come from a separate hand-eye calibration. Both describe one mounting at one time. The reprojection residual is therefore a calibration test repeated every session, not a setup step performed once.

Zhang, Zhengyou. 2000. “A Flexible New Technique for Camera Calibration.” IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence 22 (11): 1330–34. https://doi.org/10.1109/34.888718.

To detect this silent drift, automated ingestion pipelines rely on kinematic reprojection verification, formulated below and evaluated across a photographic mosaic in figure 10. Let \(\GeneralizedCoordinates \in \mathbb{R}^\JointCount\) denote the joint angles latched from optical encoders at the optical exposure midpoint. Forward kinematics \(\ForwardKinematicsMap(\GeneralizedCoordinates)\) maps these angles to the 3D surface vertices of the robot’s links in the base coordinate frame, \(\BaseFramePoint \in \mathbb{R}^3\). The camera’s extrinsic spatial transformation \(\BaseToCameraTransform = \begin{bmatrix} \DirectionalCosineMatrix & \RigidTransformTranslation \\ \ZeroVector^\top & 1 \end{bmatrix} \in \SpecialEuclideanGroup\) maps base coordinates into the camera’s optical frame (\(\CameraFramePoint = \DirectionalCosineMatrix \BaseFramePoint + \RigidTransformTranslation\)). Under the pinhole camera model parameterized by intrinsic matrix \(\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{3 \times 3}\),12 link points project to homogeneous image pixel coordinates \(\HomogeneousPixelCoordinates = [\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate, 1]^\top\): \[\CameraFrameDepth \begin{bmatrix} \PixelColumnCoordinate \\ \PixelRowCoordinate \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix \left( \DirectionalCosineMatrix \, \ForwardKinematicsMap(\GeneralizedCoordinates) + \RigidTransformTranslation \right) = \begin{bmatrix} \HorizontalFocalLength & 0 & \PrincipalPointColumn \\ 0 & \VerticalFocalLength & \PrincipalPointRow \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \left( \DirectionalCosineMatrix \, \ForwardKinematicsMap(\GeneralizedCoordinates) + \RigidTransformTranslation \right),\] where \(\CameraFrameDepth\) is the point’s depth along the camera’s optical axis, the scale that division removes when the projection \(\operatorname{proj}_{\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix}\) maps a camera-frame point to its pixel. An automated check run on every recording session evaluates the reprojection residual between the projected link wireframe and the observed physical arm in the image: \[\ReprojectionResidual = \left\| \ObservedPixelCoordinates - \operatorname{proj}_{\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix}\left( \BaseToCameraTransform \, \ForwardKinematicsMap(\GeneralizedCoordinates) \right) \right\|_2. \tag{9}\] If mechanical vibration, fastener relaxation, or an accidental impact displaces the camera bracket by even \(\FiniteChange \RigidTransformTranslation = 3\text{ mm}\) or tilts it by extrinsic misalignment \(\FiniteChange \CameraMountTiltAngle = 0.5^\circ\), the reprojection residual \(\ReprojectionResidual\) rises from its calibrated baseline of roughly one pixel to a large geometric offset. The focal length and working distance determine the size of that offset. While the underlying HDF5 or Zarr arrays remain syntactically flawless, an end-to-end visuomotor policy trained on shifted frames learns an erroneous spatial mapping between pixel coordinates and the end-effector tool center point (TCP). During closed-loop autonomous deployment, the policy issues reaching commands that miss physical targets by the uncompensated calibration offset, converting valid demonstrations into persistent latching jams or collisions with fixtures.

Figure 10 displays this verification pipeline across a \(7 \times 7\) grid of workspace scenes from the DROID dataset. In each, a colored silhouette of the robot arm, rendered from its Unified Robot Description Format (URDF) kinematic mesh at the recorded joint angles, is projected through each camera’s extrinsic calibration matrix and overlaid directly onto the raw photographic frame. Where the mask and the physical arm coincide, camera extrinsics and joint encoders are geometrically aligned; where the colored silhouette departs from the links, the check exposes camera mount slippage before corrupted trajectory pairs enter training. The DROID corpus showed how easily mount drift escapes standard checks. Although its cameras were initially calibrated with checkerboard targets during data collection, independent re-analysis revealed that rendered kinematic masks barely overlapped visible robot links across numerous recorded episodes (Lu et al. 2025). The DROID research team subsequently evaluated stored extrinsics by measuring mask intersection-over-union, discarded failed sessions, re-estimated camera poses using learning-based hand-eye estimators, and published updated calibrations for approximately 36,000 episodes (Khazatsky et al. 2024). This calibration error shipped within a major open-source corpus because it was transparent to standard file format parsers. To prevent these silent failures, ingestion architectures must bind every recorded episode to an immutable calibration identifier and real-time clock synchronization metrics. Trajectories collected under conflicting geometric or temporal calibrations force neural policies to average incompatible sensorimotor distributions within identical state spaces.

Lu, Jingpei, Zekai Liang, Tristin Xie, Florian Richter, Shan Lin, Sainan Liu, and Michael C. Yip. 2025. “CtRNet-X: Camera-to-Robot Pose Estimation in Real-World Conditions Using a Single Camera.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 1914–20. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA55743.2025.11128065.

5.10.2 Sim-to-real transfer artifacts in rigid-body simulators

In a rigid-body simulator with a relaxed soft-contact solver, a scripted gripper can interpenetrate a jammed latch plate by a millimeter and slide it open without binding. A behavioral cloning policy trained on those synthetic trajectories learns to push straight through resistance that stalls the physical arm. Because synthetic data generation replaces hardware wear with numerical approximations, degradation in a simulated dataset comes from the solver’s contact model rather than from slipped camera brackets or fatigued operators. Discrete-time physics engines resolve contact constraints once per integration step, and the chosen formulation (whether strict complementarity constraints (Stewart and Trinkle 1996), convex soft-contact relaxations (Todorov et al. 2012), or penalty-based spring-damper models) sets penetration depths, slip thresholds, and drivetrain compliance. Whereas reinforcement learning agents actively exploit numerical quirks to maximize synthetic reward, behavioral cloning policies passively inherit whatever unphysical contact impulses, penetrations, or frictionless transitions the simulator produces. Co-training on simulated and physical demonstrations can nonetheless improve real-world policy success despite discrepancies between the two regimes (table 5) (Maddukuri et al. 2025). A physical ingestion pipeline cannot enforce exact contact fidelity across arbitrary geometries; its job is to preserve provenance. Every synthetic trajectory must record its simulation engine, software version, contact solver parameters, and integration time step. The scenario table also flags cells populated solely by synthetic rollouts (table 6), so downstream training and diagnostics can separate solver artifacts from hardware behavior (The sim-to-real contact exploit).

Stewart, David E., and Jeffrey C. Trinkle. 1996. “An Implicit Time-Stepping Scheme for Rigid Body Dynamics with Inelastic Collisions and Coulomb Friction.” International Journal for Numerical Methods in Engineering 39 (15): 2673–91. https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-0207(19960815)39:15<2673::AID-NME972>3.0.CO;2-I.
Todorov, Emanuel, Tom Erez, and Yuval Tassa. 2012. “MuJoCo: A Physics Engine for Model-Based Control.” IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS), 5026–33. https://doi.org/10.1109/IROS.2012.6386109.
Maddukuri, Abhiram, Zhenyu Jiang, Lawrence Yunliang Chen, Soroush Nasiriany, Yuqi Xie, Yu Fang, Wenqi Huang, et al. 2025. “Sim-and-Real Co-Training: A Simple Recipe for Vision-Based Robotic Manipulation.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2025.XXI.109.

↪ The decomposition of transfer discrepancies into dynamics, sensing, appearance, and timing channels, sized from paired command-response traces, is developed in Physical Channels of the Sim-to-Real Gap.

5.10.3 Rig-to-rig divergence across nominally identical fleets

When a worn secondary manipulator with \(0.35^\circ\) of gearbox backlash and a \(22\text{ ms}\) torque-response lag records latch demonstrations for a fleet, the human operator unconsciously adds aggressive lead compensation to overcome the deadband. Replaying or imitating those demonstrations on a newly commissioned arm with \(0.02^\circ\) of backlash drives the new arm into limit-cycle oscillations. Even among factory-identical robots of the same model number, individual units differ in gearbox wear, thermal dissipation, and motor-controller filter tunings. To keep fleet-wide pooling from mixing incompatible plants, data ingestion pipelines must bind each unit’s embodiment contract (its kinematic parameters, actuator serial numbers, controller firmware revisions, and identified dynamic models) to every recorded file. They then filter out trajectories from units that violate the target platform’s compatibility bounds.

Aggregating fleet data also exposes a second failure mode: confusing physical task failures with telemetry logging faults. A failed attempt is a complete, physically valid sequence in which the robot tried a task and missed the goal. Discarding those episodes introduces survivorship bias13 and strips the dataset of the boundary states, recovery paths, and negative outcomes needed to train stabilizing policies. A failed recording, by contrast, is an infrastructure fault, such as a dropped network packet, a torn image frame, or a ring-buffer overrun. Ingesting a corrupted recording without tracking gaps presents missing or uneven sample intervals as a continuous fixed-rate stream, corrupting finite-difference state derivatives. Data pipelines must therefore sort every session into one of three categories: a retained success, a retained physical failure, or an excluded logging fault tagged with diagnostic error codes. In the door-latching campaign, an episode halted by the 15 N contact tripwire is a valid physical failure and stays in the provenance archive, whereas a run in which the 6-axis force-torque sensor dropped packets is quarantined with its bus error code. Retaining a physical failure does not make its commands expert imitation targets; only stabilized, reviewed recovery segments or expert-relabeled states qualify under the intervention curation rules.

13 Survivorship Bias: During the Second World War, Abraham Wald of Columbia’s Statistical Research Group estimated the vulnerability of combat aircraft from the damage on the planes that came back; the planes that were hit in the most vulnerable places were the ones missing from the sample (Mangel and Samaniego 1984). A demonstration archive filtered to successes has the same hole. The attempts that reached the failure boundary are exactly the ones absent, so the archive understates how often the boundary is reached and holds no states from it.

Mangel, Marc, and Francisco J. Samaniego. 1984. “Abraham Wald’s Work on Aircraft Survivability.” Journal of the American Statistical Association 79 (386): 259–67. https://doi.org/10.1080/01621459.1984.10478038.

Even when every packet arrives intact, a fleet interface can still corrupt an archive by recording numbers in the wrong physical units or coordinate frame. Because standard schema validators check only data types and value ranges, such mismatches pass ingestion silently until a downstream consumer interprets the numbers under a different convention (war story 5.1).

War Story 5.1: Mars Climate Orbiter unit mismatch (1999)
Context: During its nine-month cruise to Mars in 1998 and 1999, the Mars Climate Orbiter fired small thrusters to desaturate its reaction wheels, and the navigation team on the ground folded the resulting velocity changes into the trajectory models used to plan each correction maneuver (Stephenson et al. 1999).

Mechanism: A ground software file (called “Small Forces” in the investigation) reported thruster impulse in pound-force-seconds (\(\text{lbf}\cdot\text{s}\)), while the trajectory models that consumed it assumed newton-seconds (\(\text{N}\cdot\text{s}\)) as the interface specification required. Because \(1\text{ lbf}\cdot\text{s} = 4.45\text{ N}\cdot\text{s}\), every desaturation event entered the models too small by a factor of \(4.45\). The values were well formed, in range, and of the expected type, so no schema check could flag them.

Impact: The accumulated error was never resolved during cruise. At orbit insertion the spacecraft passed about \(57\text{ km}\) above the surface instead of the planned \(226\text{ km}\) and was lost.

Response: The Mishap Investigation Board traced the loss to the missing unit conversion in the ground file. It recommended that the Mars Polar Lander project verify consistent units throughout design and operations and audit all data transferred between JPL and Lockheed Martin Astronautics for compliance with the interface specification, along with stronger navigation review and anomaly escalation.

Systems lesson: A number carries its meaning only together with its units and its frame. A robot dataset runs the same risk on every action tap and every calibration. A record that stores a value without the units, frame, and calibration identifier it was produced under lets a mismatched rig pass every tensor check, which is why the provenance record of section 5.11 binds each tap to them.

Stephenson, Arthur G. et al. 1999. Mars Climate Orbiter Mishap Investigation Board Phase i Report. National Aeronautics; Space Administration.

5.10.4 Operational design domain blind spots and unsafe interpolation

If the warehouse manipulator contacts the cage door during a fast 0.10 m/s approach, an unpopulated cell in its scenario table, a policy trained on the latch-recovery-02 archive can trigger the same withdraw-and-reseat motion that worked during a slow 0.03 m/s approach. The motion yanks the gripper back before impact strain has dissipated and bends the latch pin. Encountering an unobserved operational state does not make a neural controller pause; the network continues emitting commands, often replaying familiar recovery primitives in regimes where their physical preconditions fail. Each recovery primitive therefore needs its own scenario-table coverage for the physical states in which it executes, rather than a log of the failure conditions that trigger it. Collection supplies experience, and closed-loop evaluation must establish competence in the conditions where the policy is to operate. A qualified permission restriction or physical constraint must exclude regimes that remain unsupported. The 2023 Cruise pullover recounted in When the Responsibilities Fail Together is the same failure at road scale. A familiar maneuver, the pullover toward a minimal-risk condition, ran in a state no one had covered, with a pedestrian beneath the vehicle (The four rungs of the fallback ladder gives the full account).

Sensor calibrations, human operator habits, actuator wear states, and operational design domain boundaries define the physical semantics of every recorded trajectory. If data ingestion pipelines strip these physical metadata to generate flat, uniform tensors, downstream evaluations cannot determine whether an autonomous failure stems from model capacity limitations or from unmodeled distribution shifts embedded within the training corpus. The structured provenance schema of section 5.11 defines the mechanisms that preserve this physical context throughout the dataset lifecycle.

5.11 The Dataset Schema

Consider an audit of two trajectory files from the latch campaign, each holding the exact same number of state-action pairs logged at the same frequency. File A records trajectories from four authorized operators across several days, with no permission-path clamps and consistent fingertip pads throughout. File B records a single operator, logs 620 steps where safety logic clamped joint velocities, and spans an unannounced force-sensor re-zeroing midway through the session. A standard validation script checking only tensor dimensions and value ranges accepts both files without complaint. Treating them as interchangeable training data introduces silent, corrupting bugs. File B requires explicit intervention attribution and recalibration metadata before an engineer can interpret its action labels correctly. Neither file’s tensor geometry reveals which physical latch conditions the recorded data actually covers.

The dataset schema organizes the contextual evidence required to make ingestion decisions safely. It preserves the physical origin and timing of every signal, logs collection costs and dropouts, and documents which physical conditions the archive covers or omits. These fields reflect the mechanical and operational realities of the collection cell. While complete metadata cannot guarantee that a learned policy will operate successfully in closed loop, omitting it ensures that downstream pipelines will misinterpret physical signals.

The provenance record is the immutable, machine-readable artifact bound directly to a demonstration archive. It links every recorded trajectory to the specific plant, sensor calibrations, component wear state, four action taps, collection denominators, and scenario-table join (\(\OccupiedScenarioCellCount / \RequiredScenarioCellCount\)), including its count unit, eligibility and retention rule, and minimum, under which data collection occurred. Standard tensor shapes and training loss curves cannot validate a physical dataset. By recording an archive’s empirical boundaries, the provenance record allows the policy manifest (the record that training attaches to each candidate policy) and the safety case to distinguish demonstrated historical experience from the operational competence that physical testing must establish. Datasheets for datasets (Gebru et al. 2021) standardize documentation for static corpora; embodied archives additionally require tracking physical wear, permission-path interventions, and tap histories across the causal boundary. This record must travel with the trajectory data itself. Because detachable sidecar files easily separate from their payloads during format conversions, engineers serialize the record as an immutable, hashed header within the container’s root metadata, such as an MCAP top-level metadata record or a Zarr root attribute dictionary.

↪ The sixteen-record chain, in which the provenance record is the third record, is developed in The record register.

The provenance record chains Part I’s hardware records directly to Part II’s training and evaluation records. It inherits the actuator and thermal bounds from the limit record and the clock domains and jitter budgets from the handoff record, while the proposal boundary determines which entity held control at each of the four action taps. As episodes are logged, the collection runtime adds the plant identifier, calibration matrices, component wear counts, yield denominators, and scenario-table join. The runtime then hands that complete record forward to the policy manifest, so closed-loop evaluation can test the policy against the exact conditions its data covered.

↩︎ The handoff record, which extends each limit with the handoff and clock domain that locate it in the machine, is established in The Handoffs Matrix.

Every trajectory admitted into the training corpus must identify its collection source, collector policy, and execution authority. For human teleoperation, the record logs the operator identifier, the interface hardware, and transport timing characteristics, for example a 6-DoF leader arm sampled at \(100\text{ Hz}\) with \(0.8\text{ ms}\) of input jitter. Storing an operator identifier, or capturing human faces on peripheral camera streams, makes an episode a record of people as well as of the robot. The schema therefore documents the legal and institutional basis under which each person was recorded, the authorized deployment purposes, and strict retention schedules. This governance ensures that an episode collected to teach latch insertion is not repurposed to evaluate the worker who demonstrated it. When data originates from an autonomous or mixed controller, the record stores the exact policy checkpoint, the planner version, and the active enforcement configuration, including Cartesian bounding boxes, joint velocity limits, and contact-force clamping thresholds. Trajectories must also record the active retention rule, documenting whether the runtime committed an episode automatically upon task completion, retained it after explicit operator review, or flagged it for inclusion as an instructive failure recovery.

↪ Purpose limits and retention schedules for records that capture people are enforced in Recorded people as a standing condition.

A physical machine cannot be identified by a generic catalog name or model string. In real hardware, two nominally identical robot arms behave differently because of manufacturing tolerances and component aging, so the data record specifies the individual plant through its intrinsic and extrinsic sensor calibrations, embodiment contract (section 5.9.1), motor-drive firmware revisions, and component wear state. Even replaceable contact surfaces alter the governing dynamics. A parallel gripper fitted with compliant silicone pads of Shore 30A hardness (a durometer scale for rubbers, on which higher numbers are stiffer) produces vastly different friction and compliance than the same mechanism fitted with worn Shore 60A polyurethane pads under identical joint commands. Maintenance milestones such as cable retensioning, drive-belt replacements, gearbox swaps, and camera remounts therefore belong directly in the machine state record. If technicians recalibrated joint offsets between morning and afternoon collection shifts, the dataset exposes that boundary, so downstream imitation learning does not attempt to resolve two conflicting kinematic mappings into a single policy.

Collection denominators reconcile every physical attempt as a retained success, a retained failure, or an excluded recording, while exposing the wall-clock time spent resetting the scene. Alongside those yield counts, the four action taps of section 5.5.1, override flags, and exclusion codes preserve the critical distinction between demonstrated behavior and permission-path intervention. When safety logic intervenes to clamp velocity or limit contact forces, the data pipeline must attribute those adjustments correctly rather than misinterpreting supervisory interventions as expert demonstrations.

The scenario join records dataset-supported cells (configurations containing retained demonstrations) and catalogs known absences where no empirical data exists. The count-policy digest binds the count unit, eligibility and retention rule, and declared minimum; when a minimum is specified, the record distinguishes occupied cells from those meeting it. Here, dataset support reflects historical logging coverage and must not be conflated with the evaluated support of a deployed policy. For the latch campaign, the record below connects collection cost to coverage; table 6 maps its field groups to the decisions they support.

  • Denominators: 52 attempts per scheduled session at a net retention rate of 0.68, so 5,000 retained successful demonstrations cost 7,353 attempts over 141.4 h of scheduled cell time.
  • Retention rule: an attempt whose contact force crosses the 15 N tripwire triggers the qualified collection response and is kept as a labeled failure, outside the demonstration count, when its telemetry is valid; a recording that fails quality filtering is excluded with its exclusion code.
  • Scenario table: plate offset (nominal, +3 mm, −3 mm) crossed with approach speed (0.03 m/s, 0.10 m/s), six cells, held with the count and retention policy in the record as a digest rather than as a copy.
  • supported_cells: the nominal plate at 0.03 m/s, the only cell the original retained demonstrations occupy.
  • known_absences: the other five cells, every displaced plate and every approach at 0.10 m/s.

The policy manifest of The Policy Manifest cites each contributing archive by digest, including latch-recovery-02 in the stipulated follow-up branch. The original record continues to describe the original campaign; new experience receives new provenance rather than rewriting old absences. Operator and override history also informs supervisory evaluation, while coverage and calibration evidence contributes to the release argument. Binding the metadata to the trajectory container helps preserve these relationships through ingestion, but cryptographic hashes do not automatically prevent misuse. Data loaders and ingestion pipelines must verify that trajectory arrays remain bound to their declared schema and calibration headers before training begins, because skipping any part of that check leads to the recurring collection failures cataloged next (section 5.12).

↪ The release manifest, which binds this provenance record and its operating-domain coverage into the release argument, is developed in The Release Manifest.

5.12 Fallacies and Pitfalls

In pure software systems, data collection can often treat storage growth as an unvarnished positive, since more web pages, text tokens, or labeled images generally broaden coverage. In physical artificial intelligence, however, datasets are tightly coupled to the mechanical dynamics, calibration state, and physical limits of the collection hardware. A dataset that passes every structural schema check, with zero dropped packets and consistent tensor dimensions, can still leave a deployed machine entirely blind to critical operational states. The operational distribution of physical data reflects three intertwined factors: the collecting policy (whether driven by human teleoperation or automated planning), the physical capabilities and wear of the collection rig, and the curation filters applied before admission to the archive. Neglecting these physical constraints leads to predictable engineering fallacies and costly operational pitfalls.

Fallacy: Massive sample volume compensates for lack of operational state-space coverage.

Suppose a surface-tracking policy must operate reliably across 500 distinct combinations of normal force, surface friction, and contact angle, but the archive covers only 45. Logging another collection run that adds \(72{,}000\) control steps by repeating nominal trajectories through those same 45 states inflates the database, but it leaves physical coverage unchanged. High-frequency sampling in familiar regions merely records the same dynamic trajectory more densely, adding zero evidence about the unvisited friction regimes or steep contact angles. Ingestion pipelines that report growing data volume and zero dropped packets can create a false sense of security while leaving downstream policies brittle. A collection campaign earns its cost by addressing a declared coverage, label-quality, or evidence deficit. A populated cell may still need more or better demonstrations, while nominal repetition alone supplies no evidence about unvisited regimes.

Architect’s countermeasure: Gate dataset admission on a support envelope relational join against an explicit scenario table, rather than step counts or raw byte volume. Require that new data collection campaigns specify which coverage, label-quality, or evidence deficit they target, and track scenario coverage (\(\OccupiedScenarioCellCount / \RequiredScenarioCellCount\)) as a primary engineering KPI alongside validation loss.

Pitfall: Assuming physical demonstration labels survive mechanical or contact hardware maintenance.

Archived demonstrations do not retain physical validity when the underlying hardware changes, even if array shapes and data schemas remain identical. Consider what happens when a technician replaces a parallel gripper’s compliant fingertips with stiffer pads and re-torques an adjacent camera bracket. The historical dataset still passes every automated pipeline check: sensor channels, array dimensions, and datatypes match expected specifications. If the camera bracket shifts, a known transformation matrix allows mathematical reprojection of visual coordinates. Contact mechanics, however, cannot be reprojected geometrically. The stiffer pads alter normal compliance and contact friction, causing grasp slip at substantially lower shear thresholds than the compliant material tolerated. Telemetry recorded before the swap captures the physical behavior of an obsolete body; training a closed-loop force controller on those historical tactile profiles will cause grasping failures on the modified hardware. Data provenance must track the full mechanical and calibration state of the collection rig so that any hardware alteration immediately triggers targeted review and recollected baselines.

Architect’s countermeasure: Bind every trajectory file to an immutable hardware state hash that captures camera extrinsics, motor serial numbers, and contact surface material specifications. When maintenance events alter contact stiffness or camera mountings, invalidate historical tactile and force labels for that cell until an automated revalidation protocol verifies dynamic equivalence.

Pitfall: Filtering out aborted safety interventions to sanitize the demonstration dataset.

Purging aborted demonstrations to produce a flawless training set introduces severe survivorship bias into physical policies. Consider an automated peg-insertion pipeline spanning 1,000 recorded attempts. Of these, 180 runs triggered a safety cutoff when contact forces reached or exceeded a \(15.0\text{ N}\) trip threshold, leaving 820 nominal runs that succeeded with an 82 percent yield. A curation script discards the 180 aborted and recovery episodes, preserving only the smooth runs where forces stayed comfortably below \(8.0\text{ N}\). When deployed in production, minor surface misalignment inevitably pushes an insertion force to \(9.5\text{ N}\). Although this force remains below the \(15.0\text{ N}\) trip threshold, the policy encounters a state outside its training support. Because the sanitization script erased every observation between nominal tracking and failure, the archive supplies no supervision for these near-boundary dynamics, so recovery behavior remains unsupported. Pruning near-boundary and intervention data strips away the exact state-action pairs the policy needs to regain stability after a disturbance.

Architect’s countermeasure: Implement three-way intervention curation at data ingestion. Quarantine learner commands between divergence (\(\DivergenceInstant\)) and takeover (\(\TakeoverInstant\)) for safety auditing only. Preserve the visited boundary states for expert relabeling via corrective aggregation (DAgger). Admit the subsequent human recovery as an expert sequence only after the operator’s measured stabilization (\(\OperatorStabilizationInstant\)), masking the blend and reorientation that precede it.

Pitfall: Merging demonstrations collected across kinematically asymmetric leader interfaces.

Merging trajectories across disparate teleoperation input interfaces can quietly bake kinematic dead zones into the dataset. Suppose two operators collect snap-fit assembly demonstrations using different leader devices to puppet identical follower arms. The first leader mechanism features detented wrist mechanics that restrict roll to two discrete positions (\(\LeaderWristRollAngle \in \{0.0^\circ, 90.0^\circ\}\)). The second leader uses a continuous gimbal that mechanically limits roll travel to \([-15.0^\circ, +15.0^\circ]\). Merging the two collections yields 4,000 episodes and increases overall sample volume, but neither interface can express orientations between \(20.0^\circ\) and \(70.0^\circ\). Even though the follower arm can navigate that full range, the combined archive contains zero training targets within this \(50.0^\circ\) gap. Pooling data from different input devices cannot fill gaps that neither device can mechanically traverse. Downstream imitation policies trained on the combined dataset will fail whenever initial conditions or part tolerances demand intermediate orientations.

Architect’s countermeasure: Apply the cross-embodiment compatibility contract to the leader interface itself, checking before recording that the leader and follower share the required reachable poses, joint motion ranges, and ability to move in the required directions. Enforce continuous orientation coverage checks across task-space degrees of freedom during data QA to detect mechanical dead-zones.

Fallacy: Physical data collection scales linearly with labor hours just like internet scraping scales with compute.

Treating physical data collection as an easily parallelized, linear process like web scraping ignores the mechanical entropy and human fatigue inherent to physical experimentation. Teams often extrapolate campaign timelines from early pilot runs, for example by projecting that an initial pace of 50 successful episodes per operator-hour will scale linearly across weeks of shifts. In reality, continuous operation degrades both hardware and teleoperator consistency. Repetitive contact impacts cause backlash and tooth wear in strain-wave gearboxes, thermal expansion and sensor drift cause uncalibrated joint encoders to drift away from physical ground truth, and human fatigue introduces latency spikes and jerky trajectories. As wear accumulates, the proportion of episodes passing quality filtering steadily drops, even while scheduled labor hours remain constant. Pilot runs conducted on pristine hardware systematically overstate long-term collection yield. Campaign planning must budget for routine recalibration, mechanical maintenance, ergonomic rests, and discarded runs alongside raw operator hours.

Architect’s countermeasure: Budget collection campaigns in net validated demonstration yield (\(\UsableDemonstrationYield = \lfloor \ActiveMotionDuration / \AttemptCycleDuration \rfloor \cdot \DemonstrationRetentionRate\)) and component fatigue cycles (\(\RatedFatigueLife\) ratings for strain-wave gears and internal flex harnesses), rather than gross scheduled operator hours. Estimate the overhead multiplier from the campaign’s calibration, reset, retention, and rest assumptions; validate it against sustained collection yield.

5.13 Summary

A physical machine manufactures its own training data, so every record carries the loop that produced it: the operator’s request, the permission path’s admission, and the body’s response, all under the machine’s current calibration and wear. Collecting that data is costly in a way scraping is not. In the latch campaign, only 24.6 percent of each scheduled hour survives as retained demonstration motion once startup, calibration, operator rest, resets, failed attempts, and quality filtering are paid for, and the wrist’s flex cable consumes the larger fraction of its assumed fatigue rating. Because every attempt spends cell time and component life whether or not it is kept, and because correlated control steps hold few independent samples, a campaign earns its cost by addressing a declared coverage, label-quality, or evidence deficit, including inadequate evidence within a populated cell.

Which states the archive holds is a question the archive cannot answer about itself. Coverage is a join between empirical occupancy and a scenario table derived from the declared operational design domain, and against the table the door actually demands, the baseline latch archive fills one cell of six. Inside even a populated cell, a cloned policy’s first error carries it off the demonstrated corridor into states where nothing in the archive constrains its output, which is why behavioral cloning’s worst-case cost grows with the square of the horizon. Interventions supply the missing learner-visited states, but only after curation at the divergence, takeover, and stabilization timestamps separates the learner’s commands from the expert’s recovery. Aggregating expert labels on those states makes the worst-case cost linear in the horizon only while the recoverability constant stays bounded; where a mistake cannot be undone, the state belongs to the permission path, not to the dataset.

A label is only as meaningful as the tap, clock, and plant behind it. Imitating the request teaches commands the permission path would have clamped, imitating the measured response teaches compliance and backlash as intent, and pairing either with a misdated frame converts timing error into position error at the speed of the motion. Range normalization cannot carry a label to another embodiment, and a bumped camera bracket or worn pad silently changes what a stored tuple means. The provenance record binds each archive to its taps, timestamps, plant, calibration, collection denominators, and known absences, so that training and evaluation can tell demonstrated experience from evaluated competence. Table 7 summarizes the load-bearing equations introduced throughout this chapter, detailing the physical boundaries each expression defines, the architectural decisions it governs, and its formal derivation.

Key Takeaways: Physical data collection and provenance
  • Coverage measures distinct physical configurations rather than sample volume: Repeated nominal trajectories add millions of telemetry rows without expanding scenario coverage. Adjacent control steps are so strongly autocorrelated that an hour of permission-tick records holds only about 1,800 independent samples for a slowly varying statistic, and even independent samples support only the cells where they land. An engineering team must measure dataset completeness by joining logged trajectories against an explicit scenario table that spans the operational design domain, rather than relying on raw row counts or the archive’s own observed extremes.
  • Offline validation loss cannot detect coverage deficits: Because conventional train-validation splits sample from the same underlying trajectory distribution, a policy can achieve near-zero validation loss while leaving critical operational cells unvisited. Only auditing the dataset against an independent scenario table reveals these silent omissions before deployment.
  • Every action tap represents a distinct physical control signal: An operator’s commanded request, the resolved kinematic setpoint, the safety-filtered actuator command, and the measured joint response diverge whenever hardware limiters, friction, or permission guards intervene. Logging each tap alongside synchronized timestamps ensures the supervisory target remains causal and preserves unambiguous evidence of physical interventions.
  • Preserve aborted runs and interventions with rigorous causal attribution: Discarding failed attempts discards the most valuable boundary-adjacent states in the operational envelope. Autonomous commands preceding a human takeover must be archived for fault analysis and counterfactual relabeling rather than misattributed as expert demonstrations, while the subsequent corrective maneuver should be reviewed, trimmed, and stored as an explicit recovery trajectory.
  • Physical telemetry remains bound to specific hardware embodiments: While optical perspectives can be reprojected across camera geometries, dynamic properties such as joint friction, structural compliance, and gearbox backlash are embedded directly into recorded force and torque signals. Simple range normalization masks these physical discrepancies without resolving them, requiring thorough re-calibration and verification whenever data is transferred across hardware platforms.
  • Targeted recovery data broadens operational competence under new provenance: An immutable dataset records the boundary conditions of a specific collection campaign, but engineers can systematically expand policy capabilities by appending verified recovery sequences under separate provenance tags. Asserting an expanded operational claim, however, requires end-to-end closed-loop validation on the physical machine under those target conditions.

Each latch archive in this chapter ended the same way, with a short list of supported cells and a longer list of known absences. That ratio is not a defect of one campaign. It is what the second law, endogenous data (principle 2), costs in practice. A machine whose actions choose its next states can only record the states its collecting policy chose, and every additional state it records is paid for in cell time and component life. The known absences in the provenance record are the price list. They say which experience the machine never bought, and therefore which claims no amount of training on this archive can support.

Read that way, the engineering question shifts from how much data a campaign collected to which absences it retired and what each one cost. The displaced-plate cell that latch-recovery-02 fills moves from the absence list to the supported list under new provenance. The faster approach, which no one collected, stays on the list, and the permission path must keep refusing it until evidence arrives.

5.14 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Demonstration in robotics began not as data collection for machine learning, but as teleoperation for hazardous environments. At Argonne National Laboratory, Raymond Goertz developed mechanically coupled master-slave manipulators to handle radioactive materials behind heavy shielding (Goertz 1949). These early systems linked the operator’s handle directly to remote mechanical tongs through cables and counterweights; because the transmission was entirely mechanical, contact forces against objects reflected instantly and passively back to the operator’s hand.

The systems challenge shifted radically when electrical servos and digital communication networks replaced mechanical linkages. Introducing transmission latency into the bilateral loop between the operator interface and the actuated follower can inject artificial energy into the feedback channel, quickly driving the coupled system into violent instability. Anderson and Spong (1989) proved that even small transmission delays can destabilize a bilateral teleoperator and established a framework that preserves delayed-channel passivity under its constant-delay model. Stability of the complete loop also depends on the connected systems and their interconnection conditions. Niemeyer and Slotine (1991) reformulated this remedy through wave variables, transforming power variables (force and velocity) into traveling wave representations that behave as passive transmission lines under the constant-delay model. In their comprehensive survey, Hokayem and Spong (2006) trace the progression from Goertz’s early linkages to modern delay-robust control schemes. Every teleoperation data-collection interface operates under the compromise these works describe, balancing faithful haptic transparency against the conditions needed to preserve passivity. The physical trajectories, contact transitions, and actuation commands captured in an embodied training archive inherently reflect the compromises of the collection hardware.

Synthesizing feedback controllers directly from recorded demonstrations followed an equally grounded trajectory. In one of the earliest end-to-end vision-based driving systems, ALVINN mapped camera frames to steering angles for an autonomous van (Pomerleau 1989, 1991). ALVINN immediately ran into the recovery gap. Because expert drivers kept the van centered within the lane, the recorded dataset contained virtually no examples of drifting off course or recovering from road shoulders. Pomerleau resolved this issue through an early form of data augmentation, synthetically warping camera images and computing the corresponding corrective steering angles to teach the network how to return to the road center.

Ross, Stéphane, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2010. “Efficient Reductions for Imitation Learning.” Proceedings of the Thirteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 9: 661–68.
Kelly, Michael, Chelsea Sidrane, Katherine Driggs-Campbell, and Mykel J. Kochenderfer. 2019. “HG-DAgger: Interactive Imitation Learning with Human Experts.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 8077–83. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2019.8793698.
Laskey, Michael, Jonathan Lee, Roy Fox, Anca Dragan, and Ken Goldberg. 2017. “DART: Noise Injection for Robust Imitation Learning.” Proceedings of the Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 78: 143–56.

As the field matured, Argall et al. (2009) unified robot learning from demonstration into a comprehensive framework, formalizing how data collection methods shape policy representations and analyzing the kinematic correspondence problem between the demonstrator and the physical robot. The theoretical mechanics of compounding execution errors were established by Ross and Bagnell (2010), who proved that naive behavioral cloning yields an error bound that grows quadratically with the task horizon (\(\CovariateDriftBound\)) under covariate shift. To escape this quadratic penalty, Ross et al. (2011) introduced Dataset Aggregation (DAgger), demonstrating that rolling out the learner while querying the expert along the induced state distribution achieves an optimal linear bound (\(\mathcal{O}(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\SingleStepImitationError)\)), provided the environment satisfies a finite recoverability condition. Practical robotics deployments adapted this theoretical machinery to human supervisors who cannot label millions of counterfactual query states. Human-gated DAgger (Kelly et al. 2019) transferred control to a human expert only during critical trajectory deviations, while DART (Laskey et al. 2017) injected continuous stochastic noise into the actuators during teleoperation to force human demonstrators to execute recovery maneuvers naturally. The empirical intervention strategies and recovery curation workflows in section 5.8 trace directly to these foundational methods.

The accounting of state-space coverage and sampling validity borrows from older statistical and control foundations. Bellman (1957) named the curse of dimensionality to describe the exponential explosion of discrete state spaces in dynamic programming. In environmental time series, climatologists demonstrated that serially correlated records dramatically reduce the effective degrees of freedom, requiring explicit adjustments to compute true sample sizes (Thiébaux and Zwiers 1984). Simultaneously, Abraham Wald’s wartime analysis of returning aircraft showed that evaluating only surviving samples systematically blinds the observer to critical failure modes (Mangel and Samaniego 1984).

Walke, Homer Rich, Kevin Black, Tony Z. Zhao, Quan Vuong, Chongyi Zheng, Philippe Hansen-Estruch, Andre Wang He, et al. 2023. “BridgeData V2: A Dataset for Robot Learning at Scale.” Proceedings of the 7th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 229: 1723–36.
Khazatsky, Alexander, Karl Pertsch, Suraj Nair, Ashwin Balakrishna, Sudeep Dasari, Siddharth Karamcheti, Soroush Nasiriany, et al. 2024. “DROID: A Large-Scale in-the-Wild Robot Manipulation Dataset.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2024.XX.120.

Robot learning confronted all three phenomena as datasets evolved from isolated laboratory demonstrations into massive multi-robot corpora (Walke et al. 2023; Khazatsky et al. 2024; Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al. 2024). While cross-institution pooling supplies visual and environmental variations that no single robotic cell can generate, it also pools mismatched actuator dynamics, unmodeled camera latencies, and latent operator selection biases. Standardizing such corpora requires both an explicit compatibility contract across kinematics and a rigorous accounting of unobserved conditions. Although dataset documentation frameworks (Gebru et al. 2021) originally addressed provenance and social biases in digital corpora, physical robotics demands tracking hardware-level ground truths. The physical dataset schema and provenance architecture detailed in section 5.11 extend these documentation principles to capture actuator tap points, calibration matrices, dynamic delays, and known boundary omissions.

The following readings chart the progression from classical bilateral teleoperation to modern pooled robot corpora:

  • Hokayem and Spong (2006) provides an exhaustive survey of bilateral teleoperation control, charting the historical transition from mechanically coupled manipulators to passivity-based and wave-variable architectures. It explains the core control-theoretic tension between transparent force reflection and robust stability when non-negligible communication delays separate the operator from the robot.
  • Pomerleau (1991) presents a compact, highly practical diagnosis of the recovery gap and compounding errors in end-to-end behavioral cloning. The paper details how synthetic visual transformations of road images generated corrective training labels, demonstrating the necessity of off-trajectory recovery data decades before formal distribution-shift theory was codified.
  • Argall et al. (2009) synthesizes robot learning from demonstration into a foundational taxonomy based on demonstration collection mechanisms and policy derivation techniques. It highlights the correspondence problem across differing human and robotic kinematic structures, which section 5.9 formalizes as a strict cross-embodiment mapping contract.
  • Ross et al. (2011) introduces the Dataset Aggregation (DAgger) algorithm and establishes the formal reduction of imitation learning to online convex optimization. It proves that querying an expert along the learner’s visited trajectory distribution converts the quadratic error growth of behavioral cloning into a linear bound, defining the critical recoverability constant presented in equation 8.
  • Open X-Embodiment Collaboration et al. (2024) documents the Open X-Embodiment initiative, pooling over one million trajectories across dozens of robot platforms and institutions. The paper is essential reading for systems engineers, illustrating both the positive transfer enabled by scale and the severe architectural challenges of normalizing disparate action spaces, sampling rates, and sensor configurations.
  • Gebru et al. (2021) establishes the foundational methodology for documenting dataset motivation, composition, collection workflows, and intended use. Contrasting this general framework with table 6 clarifies the specialized physical, electrical, and dynamical metadata required to safely train and deploy embodied controllers.
Argall, Brenna D., Sonia Chernova, Manuela Veloso, and Brett Browning. 2009. “A Survey of Robot Learning from Demonstration.” Robotics and Autonomous Systems 57 (5): 469–83. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.robot.2008.10.024.
Open X-Embodiment Collaboration, Abby O’Neill, Abdul Rehman, Abhinav Gupta, Abhiram Maddukuri, Abhishek Gupta, et al. 2024. “Open X-Embodiment: Robotic Learning Datasets and RT-X Models.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 6892–903. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA57147.2024.10611477.
Gebru, Timnit, Jamie Morgenstern, Briana Vecchione, Jennifer Wortman Vaughan, Hanna Wallach, Hal Daumé III, and Kate Crawford. 2021. “Datasheets for Datasets.” Communications of the ACM 64 (12): 86–92. https://doi.org/10.1145/3458723.

What’s Next: From demonstrations to policies
A policy trained on an archive whose provenance record lists more absences than supported cells learns strictly from those supported cells; its training score gives no sign of where the absences begin. The baseline latch archive covers only the nominal strike plate at the guarded approach. Policy Training opens with two candidates built on it: latch-bc-01, cloned from those demonstrations, and latch-sim-02, distilled from a simulator teacher that learned the fast approach no one collected on the machine. Each improves in training while hiding a dependency the hardware will expose. That chapter then carries the curated latch-recovery-02 archive into a recovery candidate and writes each candidate’s evidence into a policy manifest whose declared operating domain cannot exceed the cells its provenance supports. Closed-Loop Evaluation then tests whether that declared domain holds on the machine.
